/* ---- the background ----------------------------------------------------
   Four drifting colour fields plus a fine grain. All CSS: nothing to download,
   nothing to decode, and it costs one composited layer rather than a canvas
   running a loop. Tune the palette with --aur-1..4 and the pace with --drift. */
:root{
  --aur-1:#6D5BFF; --aur-2:#B14BFF; --aur-3:#00C2FF; --aur-4:#FF4BA6;
  --aur-5:#4B7BFF; --aur-6:#8A2BFF;
  --aur-alpha:.72; --drift:30s;
}
html[data-theme="dark"]{
  --aur-alpha:.66;
}
.aurora{
  position:fixed; inset:-30vh -25vw; z-index:0; pointer-events:none;
  filter:blur(78px) saturate(175%); opacity:var(--aur-alpha);
  transition:opacity .6s var(--ease);
}
.aurora i{ position:absolute; display:block; border-radius:50%; will-change:transform }

/* Six fields rather than four, with two of them anchored over the middle of
   the screen. With everything parked at the corners the centre stays white,
   which is what made the colour look like a border instead of a background. */
.aurora i:nth-child(1){ width:62vw;height:62vw; left:-10vw; top:-12vh;
  background:radial-gradient(closest-side,var(--aur-1),transparent);
  animation:drift1 var(--drift) ease-in-out infinite alternate }
.aurora i:nth-child(2){ width:58vw;height:58vw; right:-12vw; top:-16vh;
  background:radial-gradient(closest-side,var(--aur-2),transparent);
  animation:drift2 calc(var(--drift) * 1.28) ease-in-out infinite alternate }
.aurora i:nth-child(3){ width:54vw;height:54vw; left:8vw; bottom:-22vh;
  background:radial-gradient(closest-side,var(--aur-3),transparent);
  animation:drift3 calc(var(--drift) * 1.62) ease-in-out infinite alternate }
.aurora i:nth-child(4){ width:48vw;height:48vw; right:2vw; bottom:-14vh;
  background:radial-gradient(closest-side,var(--aur-4),transparent);
  animation:drift4 calc(var(--drift) * 1.9) ease-in-out infinite alternate }
.aurora i:nth-child(5){ width:56vw;height:56vw; left:22vw; top:16vh;
  background:radial-gradient(closest-side,var(--aur-5),transparent);

   8-bit displays; the noise breaks the bands up. */

  content:''; position:fixed; inset:0; z-index:0; pointer-events:none; opacity:.5;

  mix-blend-mode:overlay;
}

/* ---- topic roulette ---------------------------------------------------- */
/* The reel is a tall column of rows translated upward. Driving it from JS
   rather than CSS keyframes is what lets it decelerate on real momentum and
   still land exactly on a chosen row. */
.reel{
  grid-area:1/1; width:100%; will-change:transform;
  /* No CSS transition: every frame is positioned by script. */
}
.reel .row{
  display:flex; align-items:center; justify-content:center; text-align:center;
  padding:0 4px; font-weight:680; letter-spacing:-.035em; line-height:1.06;
  font-size:clamp(30px,7.2vw,74px);
  color:var(--ink);
}
/* Rows away from the centre fade out, so the eye is drawn to the middle
   and the reel reads as a wheel rather than a scrolling list. */
.reelmask{
  grid-area:1/1; width:100%; height:100%; overflow:hidden; position:relative;
  -webkit-mask-image:linear-gradient(180deg,transparent,#000 26%,#000 74%,transparent);
  mask-image:linear-gradient(180deg,transparent,#000 26%,#000 74%,transparent);
}
.spinning .eyebrow{opacity:.5}

  <div class="head"><h1>About</h1>
    <p>There are thousands of things you could learn. The hard part was never finding
       the time — it was choosing.</p></div>
  <div class="stats" id="stats"></div>
  <h2>What this is</h2>
  <p>A short, honest explanation of one idea, sized so you could finish it in a lunch
     break. Every topic here can be genuinely understood at a basic level in under an
     hour — not skimmed, understood.</p>
  <h2>How to use it</h2>
  <p>Press Start and let topics scroll past until one catches you. Open it, read it,
     mark it learned. Or use Surprise Me and accept whatever you get, which is a
     surprisingly good way to find things you would never have searched for.</p>
  <h2>Explain it back</h2>
  <p>At the bottom of every topic there is a button to record yourself explaining it.
     Saying an idea out loud, without the page in front of you, is the fastest honest
     test of whether you actually understood it — the place you stumble is the part you
     did not. Clips are kept in your <b>Gallery</b>.</p>
  <h2>Your progress</h2>
  <p>What you mark as learned is stored in this browser only, and so are your
     recordings. Nothing is uploaded, there are no accounts, and clearing your browser
     data clears both — so download any clip you want to keep.</p>
  <h2>Keyboard</h2>
  <p><kbd>↑</kbd><kbd>↓</kbd> move between topics · <kbd>Enter</kbd> open ·
     <kbd>R</kbd> random · <kbd>/</kbd> search · <kbd>Esc</kbd> back</p>
</div></div></section>

<script>
/* ============================================================================
   TOPICS — the whole content of the site.

   Every field is authored, not generated: the explanations carry real
   mechanisms, numbers and names, because a page that only rephrases its own
   title teaches nothing. To add a topic, copy one entry and fill it in; the
   category chips, search, counters and Surprise Me all read from this array,
   so nothing else needs touching.

     t     title            c    category        m  minutes to learn
     hook  one-line pitch shown under the title
     why   why it is worth an hour of your life
     body  the explanation, one string per paragraph
     ex    concrete examples
     facts things worth knowing that did not fit the explanation
     keys  [term, definition] pairs
     sum   the one thing to remember
   ========================================================================== */
const TOPICS = [
{t:'How black holes work',c:'Space',m:14,
hook:'A place where gravity wins so completely that even light cannot leave.',
why:'Black holes are where our two best theories of physics contradict each other, which makes them the most likely place to find whatever comes next.',
body:['Squeeze any mass into a small enough sphere and its escape velocity — the speed you need to fly away — exceeds the speed of light. Since nothing outruns light, nothing gets out. The boundary where that happens is the event horizon, and it is not a surface but a point of no return.',
'The Sun would need to be crushed to about 3 km across to become one. Earth would need to fit inside a marble. Stellar black holes form when a massive star runs out of fuel and its core collapses under its own weight, with nothing left to push back.',
'Inside the horizon, the geometry of space and time tilts so far that moving toward the centre becomes as unavoidable as moving forward in time. That is why "escaping" is not a matter of engine power. There is no direction that points out.',
'We see them by what they do to their neighbours: gas heated to millions of degrees as it spirals in, stars whipping around an invisible point, and gravitational waves from mergers. In 2019 the Event Horizon Telescope produced the first image of one — a ring of light around a dark centre.'],
ex:['Sagittarius A*, the 4-million-solar-mass black hole at the centre of our galaxy, was found by watching stars orbit an empty point for decades.',
'A clock near a black hole genuinely runs slow relative to yours — the same effect GPS satellites correct for, taken to an extreme.'],
facts:['Black holes are not cosmic vacuum cleaners. Replace the Sun with an equal-mass black hole and Earth would keep its orbit unchanged.',
'They evaporate. Hawking radiation means a black hole slowly leaks energy and, given absurd amounts of time, disappears.'],
keys:[['Event horizon','The boundary beyond which nothing can return.'],
['Singularity','The point where our equations give infinite density and stop being trustworthy.'],
['Accretion disk','The glowing spiral of matter falling in, which is what we usually actually see.'],
['Hawking radiation','A slow thermal leak that makes black holes finite rather than eternal.']],
sum:'A black hole is not a thing so much as a region where escape has stopped being a direction you can point in.'},

{t:'How the human brain creates memories',c:'Psychology',m:13,
hook:'Memory is not a recording. It is a reconstruction, rebuilt slightly differently each time.',
why:'Understanding that memory is rebuilt rather than replayed changes how you study, how much you trust eyewitnesses, and how much you trust yourself.',
body:['An experience arrives as a pattern of firing across many brain regions. The hippocampus binds those scattered pieces — a smell here, a face there — into one indexed event. Without it you can still learn skills, but you stop forming new episodic memories, which is what happened to the patient known as H.M. after his hippocampi were removed in 1953.',
'Consolidation moves memories from fragile to durable, largely during sleep. The hippocampus replays the day at high speed, and the cortex gradually takes ownership. This is why a night of poor sleep costs you the day before it, not just the day after.',
'Every time you recall something, it becomes briefly editable again — reconsolidation. Whatever you are feeling and believing at that moment can be written back in. Memories therefore drift toward your current story of yourself.',
'This is also why retrieval beats rereading. Pulling a fact out strengthens the path to it far more than putting it in again. Testing yourself feels harder because it is doing more work.'],
ex:['Flashbulb memories of major events feel vivid and certain, yet studies tracking them show they change substantially within a year while confidence stays high.',
'Spaced repetition works because each near-forgotten retrieval is a bigger strengthening event than an easy one.'],
facts:['London taxi drivers who memorise the city have measurably enlarged posterior hippocampi, and it grows with years on the job.',
'You cannot form a memory of something you never attended to. Most "forgetting" is really never having encoded it.'],
keys:[['Hippocampus','Binds the scattered pieces of an event into one retrievable index.'],
['Consolidation','The slow transfer from fragile to durable storage, largely during sleep.'],
['Reconsolidation','Recall reopens a memory for editing, so remembering can change it.'],
['Retrieval practice','Recalling a fact strengthens it far more than rereading it.']],
sum:'You do not store memories, you store instructions for rebuilding them — and every rebuild is a chance to change them.'},

{t:'Why we dream',c:'Psychology',m:11,
hook:'Every night your brain runs vivid simulations you did not ask for and mostly forget.',
why:'Dreaming takes years of your life. Knowing what it is for tells you something about what sleep is doing to your mind.',
body:['Most vivid dreaming happens in REM sleep, when the brain is nearly as active as when awake but the body is paralysed from the neck down — a safety interlock that stops you acting the dream out. People whose interlock fails do act them out, sometimes violently.',
'During REM, the regions handling emotion and visual imagery are highly active while the prefrontal cortex — the part that checks whether something makes sense — is quiet. That combination explains dream logic exactly: intense, vivid, and unquestioned.',
'The leading accounts are not mutually exclusive. Dreams may rehearse threats in a safe simulator, they may help consolidate and reorganise memory by replaying it in new combinations, and they may strip the emotional charge off difficult experiences while keeping the information.',
'Notably, noradrenaline — the stress chemical — is essentially absent during REM. You can revisit something painful without the physiological alarm firing, which is a plausible mechanism for why time and sleep dull emotional wounds.'],
ex:['People deprived of REM specifically, rather than sleep generally, show worse emotional regulation and poorer pattern-finding the next day.',
'The "aha" of waking with a solved problem is well documented — REM favours remote, loose associations over tight logical ones.'],
facts:['Everyone dreams; people who say they never do simply do not recall them. Waking during REM is what makes a dream memorable.',
'Blind people who lost sight early dream in sound, touch and smell rather than images.'],
keys:[['REM sleep','The stage of vivid dreaming, with an active brain and a paralysed body.'],
['Atonia','The muscle shutdown that stops you acting out a dream.'],
['Memory reorganisation','Replaying experience in novel combinations, which may be where insight comes from.']],
sum:'Dreaming looks like your brain rehearsing, reorganising and defusing the day, using a simulator with its fact-checker switched off.'},

{t:'How Wi-Fi actually works',c:'Technology',m:12,
hook:'Your laptop is talking to a box across the room using radio, and shouting politely.',
why:'Wi-Fi problems are almost never "the internet is slow" — knowing the mechanism tells you which of the five possible causes it actually is.',
body:['Wi-Fi is radio. Your device and the router convert data into patterns of radio waves at 2.4, 5 or 6 GHz. The information is encoded by varying the phase and amplitude of the wave in tiny steps, thousands of times per second, and a stronger, cleaner signal lets each step carry more bits.',
'Only one device can usefully talk at a time on a channel, so Wi-Fi uses a politeness protocol: listen first, and if the air is busy, wait a random moment and try again. This is why one slow device drags a whole network — it holds the air longer for the same amount of data.',
'2.4 GHz travels further and penetrates walls better but is crowded and has only three non-overlapping channels. 5 GHz and 6 GHz are much wider and quieter but are stopped by walls more easily. That trade — range against capacity — is the whole reason your router offers both.',
'Everything after the router is a separate problem. Wi-Fi covers the last few metres; the speed of your connection to the wider internet is set by your line, not your radio.'],
ex:['A microwave oven runs at 2.45 GHz. A leaky one genuinely does interrupt 2.4 GHz Wi-Fi while it is on.',
'A "full bars but nothing loads" phone usually has a strong radio link and a broken connection beyond the router.'],
facts:['Wi-Fi was a by-product of radio-astronomy maths for un-smearing signals, patented by Australia’s CSIRO.',
'Water absorbs 2.4 GHz well, which is why a room full of people measurably weakens a signal.'],
keys:[['Channel','A slice of frequency. Overlapping channels interfere with each other.'],
['Modulation','How data is written onto the wave; better signal means more bits per step.'],
['CSMA/CA','Listen-before-talking, the politeness rule that shares the air.'],
['Band','2.4 GHz for reach, 5/6 GHz for speed.']],
sum:'Wi-Fi is a shared radio conversation where distance buys reach, width buys speed, and everybody has to take turns.'},

{t:'How GPS knows where you are',c:'Technology',m:12,
hook:'Your phone works out its position by measuring how late four clocks are.',
why:'It is the most-used practical application of Einstein’s relativity on Earth, and it is genuinely simple once you see the trick.',
body:['Each GPS satellite carries an atomic clock and broadcasts, continuously, "this is satellite 7, and the time is exactly now". Your phone receives that message slightly late, because radio travels at the speed of light and the satellite is roughly 20,000 km away.',
'Multiply the delay by the speed of light and you have your distance from that satellite — which puts you somewhere on a sphere around it. Two satellites narrow you to a circle, three to a couple of points, and one of those is absurd (deep space or inside the Earth).',
'The complication is that your phone does not have an atomic clock, so its own idea of "now" is wrong by an unknown amount. That unknown is a fourth variable, which is why you need a fourth satellite. Solving four equations gives your position and, as a bonus, the exact time.',
'Relativity is not a footnote here. The satellites’ clocks run fast by about 45 microseconds a day from weaker gravity and slow by about 7 from their speed. The net 38 microseconds, uncorrected, would push your position off by about 10 km per day.'],
ex:['GPS gives you position, not direction. Your phone infers heading from successive positions, which is why it guesses wrong when you stand still.',
'Urban canyons degrade accuracy because signals arrive after bouncing off buildings, making the trip look longer.'],
facts:['GPS receivers are passive. Your phone never transmits to the satellites, so the system cannot tell how many people are using it.',
'The system provides the world with free, precise time, which is what synchronises power grids and financial trades.'],
keys:[['Trilateration','Fixing position from distances to known points, not angles.'],
['Clock bias','Your receiver’s unknown time error, solved for by a fourth satellite.'],
['Time dilation','Speed slows clocks, weaker gravity speeds them up; GPS corrects for both.']],
sum:'GPS turns "how late is this message?" into "where am I?", and needs a fourth satellite only because your own clock is untrustworthy.'},

{t:'How the internet actually works',c:'Technology',m:15,
hook:'There is no such thing as the internet. There is only an agreement between networks.',
why:'Almost everything you do runs on it, and the design decisions behind it explain both its resilience and its problems.',
body:['Your message is chopped into packets. Each carries a destination address and can travel independently, by any route. If one is lost, only that one is resent. This was a deliberate choice: a network with no centre survives losing any part of itself.',
'Routers do not know the whole path. Each knows only "for that destination, the next hop is that way", and passes the packet on. The route emerges from thousands of local decisions, which is why traffic reroutes automatically when a cable is cut.',
'IP moves packets and does not care whether they arrive. TCP sits on top and adds the guarantees: numbering, acknowledgements, re-sending what got lost, and slowing down when the network is congested. Separating those two jobs is the design’s masterstroke.',
'DNS is the phone book. "example.com" means nothing to a router; a chain of DNS servers turns it into an IP address first. That is why the web can seem "down" when only name lookup has failed.'],
ex:['A single page load may open dozens of connections to several companies’ servers, and the packets may take different physical routes.',
'Undersea cables carry over 95% of intercontinental traffic. Satellites are the exception, not the rule.'],
facts:['Packets often cross national borders for domestic traffic, purely because that route was cheaper or shorter.',
'The internet has no on-switch and no owner. It is a set of protocols that networks voluntarily agree to speak.'],
keys:[['Packet','A small labelled chunk of your data that travels independently.'],
['IP','Addresses and delivers packets, with no promises.'],
['TCP','Adds ordering, acknowledgement and retransmission on top of IP.'],
['DNS','Translates names people can remember into addresses routers can use.']],
sum:'The internet is millions of independent networks agreeing on how to pass labelled envelopes, with no one in charge of the route.'},

{t:'How neural networks learn',c:'AI',m:16,
hook:'Millions of numbers, nudged very slightly, millions of times, toward being less wrong.',
why:'Nearly every AI system you have heard of is this one idea at different scales. It is far simpler than the mystique suggests.',
body:['A neural network is a stack of layers. Each layer multiplies its inputs by a matrix of weights, adds a bias, and applies a simple non-linear function. That non-linearity is essential: without it, any stack of layers collapses into a single linear one and the depth buys nothing.',
'Training starts with random weights, so the first outputs are nonsense. A loss function measures exactly how wrong the output was, as one number. The whole of training is the search for weights that make that number small.',
'Backpropagation is how it finds them. It is the chain rule from calculus, applied backwards through the network, answering "if I nudged this particular weight up slightly, would the loss go up or down, and by how much?" — for every weight at once. Then every weight takes a small step downhill.',
'Repeat over millions of examples and structure emerges. Early layers in an image network learn edges, later ones textures, later still whole objects — and nobody specified any of it. That is the actual surprise: the features are discovered, not designed.'],
ex:['A network trained only on labelled photos ends up with edge detectors in its first layer that look remarkably like those in the mammalian visual cortex.',
'Overfitting is memorising the training set. The cure is more data, or deliberately handicapping the network so it cannot afford to memorise.'],
facts:['The core algorithm dates from the 1980s. What changed was data and GPUs, not the mathematics.',
'"Deep" just means many layers. There is no threshold at which a network becomes deep.'],
keys:[['Weights','The numbers that get adjusted. They are the entire learned knowledge.'],
['Loss function','A single number saying how wrong the output was.'],
['Backpropagation','Calculating each weight’s share of the blame, efficiently.'],
['Gradient descent','Taking small steps downhill on the loss.']],
sum:'Learning is measuring how wrong you were, working out which knob caused it, and turning every knob slightly — a few million times.'},

{t:'How large language models work',c:'AI',m:16,
hook:'A machine that predicts the next word, trained until prediction starts to look like understanding.',
why:'These systems are now embedded in work, school and search. Knowing what they do and do not do is basic literacy.',
body:['At the core is a startlingly simple objective: given some text, predict the next chunk. That is all the training signal is. Doing it well, however, requires the model to internalise grammar, facts, style, arithmetic patterns and something that behaves like reasoning — because all of those help prediction.',
'Text is split into tokens — roughly word-pieces — and each becomes a vector, a list of numbers positioning it in a space where related meanings sit near each other. The transformer architecture then uses attention: for each token, it works out which other tokens matter for interpreting it, and pulls in their information.',
'Attention is what unlocked scale. Unlike older sequential models it processes a whole passage in parallel and can link distant words directly, so "it" in a long paragraph can attach to the noun it refers to fifty words back.',
'After the raw prediction training comes alignment: the model is tuned on demonstrations and human preferences to be helpful and to refuse harmful requests. This changes behaviour, not knowledge. The underlying engine is still predicting continuations.'],
ex:['Ask for a citation and a model may produce one that is perfectly formatted and entirely invented, because plausible-looking is exactly what it optimises for.',
'The same mechanism explains why phrasing matters so much: you are steering a conditional probability distribution.'],
facts:['Models have no memory between conversations unless something explicitly stores and re-feeds it.',
'Tokenisation is why they historically struggled to count letters in a word: they never saw the letters, only the chunk.'],
keys:[['Token','A word-piece, the unit the model actually reads and writes.'],
['Embedding','A vector position that encodes meaning by proximity.'],
['Attention','Deciding which other words matter for interpreting this one.'],
['Hallucination','Confident, fluent output that is simply not true.']],
sum:'Predict the next token, at enormous scale, and a great deal of apparent understanding falls out — along with confident invention.'},

{t:'How compound interest works',c:'Finance',m:9,
hook:'The reason starting ten years earlier beats saving twice as much.',
why:'It is the single most useful piece of arithmetic for your own life, and almost everyone underestimates it because human intuition is stubbornly linear.',
body:['Simple interest pays on your original amount only. Compound interest pays on your original amount plus all the interest already earned, so the base itself grows. The result is exponential rather than straight-line, and exponential curves are almost impossible to eyeball correctly.',
'The formula is final = principal x (1 + r)^n, where r is the rate per period and n is the number of periods. The important part is that n is an exponent. Time is not one factor among several — it dominates everything else.',
'The Rule of 72 makes this mental. Divide 72 by the annual percentage rate to get roughly the years to double. At 6%, money doubles every 12 years; at 9%, every 8. Those few percentage points are decades of difference over a working life.',
'The same mathematics runs backwards. Credit card debt at 20% compounding is the identical curve pointed at you, which is why minimum payments can leave a balance barely moving for years.'],
ex:['Save 200 a month from 25 to 35 and then stop; at 7% you will usually finish ahead of somebody who saves the same monthly amount from 35 to 65.',
'Fees compound too. A 1% annual fee can quietly consume a quarter of a lifetime portfolio.'],
facts:['Inflation is compounding in reverse: at 3%, prices double roughly every 24 years.',
'Compounding frequency matters far less than the rate and the years. Daily versus annual is a rounding error next to starting a decade sooner.'],
keys:[['Principal','The amount you started with.'],
['Rate','Growth per period, the base of the exponent.'],
['Rule of 72','72 divided by the rate gives the years to double.'],
['Time horizon','The exponent, and the variable that matters most.']],
sum:'Growth on growth turns time into the most valuable input you have, in both directions.'},

{t:'How inflation works',c:'Economics',m:11,
hook:'Prices do not rise. Money falls.',
why:'Inflation quietly decides your pay rise, your savings, your rent and your mortgage. It is the most consequential number most people never examine.',
body:['Inflation is a fall in the purchasing power of a unit of currency, measured by pricing a fixed basket of goods over time. If the basket costs 3% more, each unit of money buys 3% less. Individual prices always move for their own reasons; inflation is about the general level.',
'Broadly there are two engines. Demand-pull: more spending chasing the same amount of goods, so sellers raise prices. Cost-push: the cost of producing things rises — energy, wages, shipping — and gets passed on. Real episodes are usually both at once.',
'Expectations then take over, and this is the part that makes inflation self-sustaining. If everyone expects 5%, workers ask for 5%, firms price in 5%, and the expectation delivers itself. This is why central banks talk so obsessively about credibility.',
'Their main lever is interest rates. Raising them makes borrowing expensive and saving attractive, which cools spending and investment, which cools prices — at the cost of slower growth and higher unemployment. That trade-off is the whole argument.'],
ex:['Inflation transfers value from lenders to borrowers: a fixed-rate mortgage is repaid in money worth less than the money borrowed.',
'Cash "at zero risk" loses value at exactly the inflation rate, guaranteed.'],
facts:['Deflation sounds appealing and is generally worse: falling prices reward waiting, so spending stalls and debts grow heavier in real terms.',
'Most central banks target about 2% rather than 0%, to keep a safety margin above deflation.'],
keys:[['CPI','The price of a fixed basket of goods, tracked over time.'],
['Demand-pull','Too much money chasing too few goods.'],
['Cost-push','Rising production costs passed on to buyers.'],
['Real vs nominal','Nominal is the number, real is after inflation. Only real matters.']],
sum:'Inflation is money losing value, driven by demand, costs and — decisively — what everyone expects next.'},

{t:'How airplanes fly',c:'Engineering',m:11,
hook:'Not because the top of the wing is longer. That explanation is wrong.',
why:'It is the most commonly mis-taught piece of everyday physics, and the real answer is more satisfying.',
body:['A wing throws air downwards. Newton\'s third law does the rest: push a large mass of air down, and it pushes you up. The wing achieves this mostly through its angle of attack — tilted slightly nose-up into the oncoming flow — and its curved shape, which persuades the air leaving the trailing edge to leave heading downward.',
'The "equal transit time" story, where air over the longer top surface must speed up to meet its partner, is simply false: the parcels never meet, and planes fly perfectly well upside down. Lift scales with air density, wing area, and the square of speed — which is why a heavy jet needs a long, fast take-off run.'],
ex:['Stick your hand out of a moving car and tilt it. That upward shove is exactly the mechanism, at a smaller scale.',
'A stall is not running out of speed but exceeding the angle of attack at which flow stays attached; the wing stops deflecting air and lift collapses.'],
facts:['Flaps increase both camber and area for slow flight, which is why they extend for landing and retract in cruise.',
'A 747 wing flexes several metres in flight, by design.'],
keys:[['Angle of attack','The wing\'s tilt into the oncoming air — the main lift control.'],['Lift','The upward reaction to air being pushed down.'],['Stall','Flow separating from the wing, causing a sudden loss of lift.']],
sum:'Wings fly by throwing air downwards; everything else is a detail of how efficiently they do it.'},

{t:'How rockets work',c:'Space',m:11,
hook:'Rockets do not push against the air. They push against the stuff they throw out of the back.',
why:'It explains why space launch is so brutally hard, and why the tyranny of the rocket equation shapes every mission ever flown.',
body:['A rocket burns propellant and expels it at high speed through a nozzle. Momentum is conserved: mass going backward fast means the rocket goes forward. Nothing is being pushed against, which is why rockets work best in vacuum, where there is no air pressure resisting the exhaust.',
'The rocket equation is unforgiving. To gain speed you need propellant, but propellant has mass, which needs more propellant to accelerate. The relationship is logarithmic, so doubling your final velocity requires far more than doubling the fuel. Staging — dropping empty tanks — exists purely to escape this trap.'],

'The Saturn V was about 85% propellant by mass at lift-off.'],
facts:['Specific impulse measures exhaust efficiency; chemical rockets top out near 450 seconds, ion engines exceed 3,000 but push very gently.',
'Most of a launch vehicle you see on the pad is thrown away in the first few minutes.'],
keys:[['Thrust','Force from expelling mass at speed.'],['Rocket equation','Velocity gain grows only with the logarithm of the mass ratio.'],['Staging','Shedding dead weight mid-flight to beat the equation.']],
sum:'Throw mass backward fast; the hard part is that the fuel to do it is itself mass you must carry.'},

{t:'How the heart pumps blood',c:'Medicine',m:10,

body:['The heart is really two pumps side by side. The right side receives oxygen-poor blood from the body and sends it the short distance to the lungs. The left side receives freshly oxygenated blood from the lungs and pushes it around the entire body, which is why its wall is far thicker.',
'Each beat starts electrically at the sinoatrial node, the heart\'s own pacemaker, which fires without any signal from the brain. The impulse spreads across the atria, pauses briefly at the AV node so the atria can finish emptying, then sweeps through the ventricles. Four one-way valves make the flow go one direction; their closing is the lub-dub you hear.'],

'Blood pressure of 120/80 is the pressure during ventricular contraction over the pressure while the heart refills.'],

keys:[['Atrium/ventricle','Receiving chamber and pumping chamber.'],['SA node','The natural pacemaker that sets the rhythm.'],['Systole/diastole','Contraction and relaxation.']],
sum:'Two pumps, four valves and a built-in electrical clock, running one circuit to the lungs and one to everything else.'},

{t:'How supply and demand work',c:'Economics',m:9,
hook:'Two curves that quietly set almost every price you have ever paid.',
why:'It is the single most useful model in economics and explains shortages, surges and queues without any maths.',
body:['Demand slopes down: as price rises, fewer people want it. Supply slopes up: as price rises, producers will make more. Where the curves cross is the market-clearing price, where the amount people want to buy equals the amount people want to sell. Away from that point there is either a queue or a pile of unsold stock, and price moves.',
'The subtlety is elasticity — how sharply quantity responds to price. Insulin is inelastic: people buy it almost regardless of price. A particular brand of crisps is elastic: raise the price and buyers switch. Elasticity, not the curves themselves, decides who bears a tax or a shock.'],
ex:['Rent controls hold price below the crossing point, which reliably produces shortages and long waiting lists rather than cheap housing for everybody.',
'Surge pricing is the mechanism working in public: price rises until demand matches the supply of drivers.'],
facts:['A tax falls hardest on whichever side is less able to walk away, regardless of who legally pays it.',
'Shortages are not caused by scarcity alone but by prices not being allowed to move.'],
keys:[['Equilibrium','The price where quantity demanded equals quantity supplied.'],['Elasticity','How much quantity responds to a change in price.'],['Shortage/surplus','What happens when price is held away from equilibrium.']],
sum:'Price is not a value judgement, it is the signal that balances how much people want against how much exists.'},

{t:'How encryption works',c:'Cybersecurity',m:13,
hook:'Maths that lets two strangers agree a secret in public, while everyone listens.',
why:'It protects your messages, money and identity, and the public-key idea is genuinely beautiful.',
body:['Symmetric encryption uses one shared key to both lock and unlock. It is fast and strong, but it has a bootstrap problem: how do you deliver the key to someone you have never met, over a channel that is being watched?',
'Public-key cryptography solves it. Each person has a pair: a public key anyone may have, and a private key never shared. Anything locked with the public key can only be opened with the private one. The security rests on operations that are easy forwards and brutally hard backwards — multiplying two large primes is trivial, factoring the product is not.',
'In practice both are used together. Public-key maths is slow, so it is used only to agree a temporary symmetric key, and the actual conversation is encrypted with that. This is exactly what happens in the first milliseconds of every HTTPS connection.'],
ex:['End-to-end encryption means the keys live on the devices, so even the service passing the messages cannot read them.',
'A padlock icon means the connection is encrypted — not that the site is honest.'],
facts:['Modern encryption is not broken by guessing keys; it is broken by stealing them, or by bugs in the surrounding code.',
'Large quantum computers would break today\'s public-key maths, which is why post-quantum algorithms are being standardised now.'],
keys:[['Symmetric key','One shared secret, fast, hard to distribute.'],['Public/private key','A pair where one locks and only the other unlocks.'],['Key exchange','Agreeing a shared secret over a public channel.']],
sum:'Slow clever maths to agree a secret in the open, then fast simple maths to keep the conversation private.'},

{t:'How passwords are stored securely',c:'Cybersecurity',m:10,
hook:'A properly run service does not know your password, and could not tell you it if you asked.',
why:'It explains what a breach actually exposes, and why some leaks are catastrophic and others merely embarrassing.',
body:['Passwords should never be stored. Instead the server stores a hash — the output of a one-way function that is easy to compute and effectively impossible to reverse. At login it hashes what you typed and compares. If the database is stolen, the attacker gets hashes, not passwords.',
'That alone is not enough. Identical passwords produce identical hashes, so attackers precompute tables of common passwords. The fix is a salt: a unique random value mixed into each password before hashing, which makes every stored hash unique and precomputation useless. Then the hash function is deliberately made slow — bcrypt, scrypt, Argon2 — so that guessing billions of candidates becomes uneconomic.'],
ex:['If a site can email you your existing password, it is storing it reversibly and you should assume it will leak.',
'Password managers help because reuse is the real danger: one breach then unlocks everything else.'],
facts:['General-purpose hashes like MD5 or SHA-256 are the wrong tool here precisely because they are fast.',
'Length beats complexity. A long passphrase outperforms a short string of symbols.'],
keys:[['Hashing','One-way transformation; you can check a password without knowing it.'],['Salt','Unique random data per password, defeating precomputed tables.'],['Key stretching','Making hashing deliberately slow to make guessing expensive.']],
sum:'Store a slow, salted hash — then a stolen database is a pile of expensive puzzles rather than a list of logins.'},

{t:'How databases work',c:'Programming',m:12,
hook:'Finding one row among a billion, in milliseconds, without looking at the others.',
why:'Almost every application is a database with a costume on. Indexes and transactions explain most performance and correctness problems.',
body:['Data lives in tables of rows and columns. Without help, finding a row means scanning every one — fine for a thousand, hopeless for a billion. An index is a separate sorted structure, usually a B-tree, that turns that scan into a handful of jumps. The trade is that every index must be updated on every write, so indexes speed reads and slow writes.',
'Transactions provide the correctness guarantees, summarised as ACID: a group of changes either all happen or none do; the data never violates its rules; concurrent transactions do not see each other half-finished; and once committed, changes survive a crash. That last one is why databases write to a log before touching the data.'],
ex:['A bank transfer must debit one account and credit another. A crash between the two must roll back, which is atomicity.',
'A query that suddenly gets slow has often outgrown an index, or lost the ability to use one.'],
facts:['NoSQL databases usually trade some of these guarantees for scale and flexibility, which is a choice rather than an upgrade.',
'Most real-world slowness is a missing index, not a slow machine.'],
keys:[['Index','A sorted side-structure that avoids full scans.'],['Transaction','A group of changes that all succeed or all fail.'],['ACID','Atomicity, consistency, isolation, durability.']],
sum:'Databases are fast because of indexes and trustworthy because of transactions, and each costs you something.'},

{t:'How computer processors work',c:'Technology',m:14,
hook:'Billions of switches, doing arithmetic on numbers, billions of times a second.',
why:'It is the machine underneath everything digital, and the core loop is astonishingly simple.',
body:['A CPU repeats one cycle: fetch the next instruction from memory, decode what it means, execute it, and store the result. Instructions are extremely primitive — add two numbers, compare them, jump elsewhere if equal. Every program you have ever run is millions of these, arranged carefully.',
'Speed comes from tricks piled on that loop. Pipelining starts the next instruction before the last has finished, like an assembly line. Multiple cores run separate streams in parallel. Caches keep recently used data in tiny fast memory next to the core, because fetching from main memory is roughly a hundred times slower — cache misses, not clock speed, dominate real performance.',
'Branch prediction guesses which way an if-statement will go and starts work early. It is usually right; when wrong, the work is thrown away. This guessing is why clock speed alone stopped predicting performance years ago.'],
ex:['A modern CPU may have 100+ instructions in flight at once across its pipeline stages.',
'Adding cores helps only if the work can be split; many tasks are stubbornly sequential.'],
facts:['Transistors in a current high-end chip number in the tens of billions, each a few nanometres across.',
'Clock speeds stopped climbing around 2005 because of heat, which is why chips went wide instead of fast.'],
keys:[['Fetch-decode-execute','The fundamental cycle.'],['Cache','Small fast memory that hides how slow RAM is.'],['Pipelining','Overlapping instruction stages like an assembly line.']],
sum:'A simple loop over primitive instructions, made fast by never waiting when it can guess or overlap instead.'},

{t:'How operating systems work',c:'Technology',m:12,
hook:'The referee that lets a hundred programs share one machine without noticing each other.',
why:'It explains multitasking, crashes, permissions and why your computer slows down when memory runs out.',
body:['An OS has one core job: manage shared resources. The scheduler gives each process a slice of CPU time, switching between them thousands of times a second, which is why a single core appears to run many programs at once. Each switch saves and restores the program\'s state, and that bookkeeping is real overhead.',
'Memory is virtualised. Every process believes it has the machine\'s whole address space to itself; the OS and hardware translate those fake addresses to real ones. This is both convenience and security — one program literally cannot address another\'s memory. When physical memory runs out, pages get written to disk, and because disk is vastly slower, this is why a machine short on RAM crawls.'],
ex:['A crashed application does not take the system down because it runs in user mode and cannot touch the kernel\'s memory.',
'File permissions are the OS deciding, on every single access, whether this process may do that.'],
facts:['The kernel is the privileged core; almost everything else, including the interface, is an ordinary program.',
'Drivers are the usual cause of full system crashes, because they run with kernel privileges.'],
keys:[['Process','A running program with its own isolated memory.'],['Scheduler','Decides who runs next and for how long.'],['Virtual memory','Every process gets its own private illusion of memory.']],
sum:'The OS hands out time, memory and hardware in slices, and enforces the walls that keep programs out of each other.'},

{t:'How solar panels work',c:'Physics',m:10,

body:['A solar cell is a sandwich of silicon treated so one layer has spare electrons and the other has spare vacancies. Where they meet, a permanent internal electric field forms. 'Efficiency is capped by physics, not just engineering. Photons with too little energy pass through, and photons with too much waste the excess as heat. That limit puts a single-junction silicon cell at about 33% in theory, and commercial panels sit near 20–23%. Stacking layers tuned to different wavelengths is how research cells go higher.'],
ex:['Panels lose efficiency as they get hot, so a cold bright day can outproduce a hot one.',

facts:['Solar module prices have fallen by roughly 99% since 1980.',
'A panel typically repays the energy used to make it within one to two years.'],
keys:[['Photovoltaic effect','Light freeing electrons in a semiconductor.'],['P-N junction','The internal field that sorts the freed charges.'],['Shockley-Queisser limit','The theoretical efficiency ceiling for a single junction.']],
sum:'Light frees electrons, a built-in field pushes them one way, and that one-way flow is your current.'},

{t:'How batteries work',c:'Chemistry',m:10,
hook:'A controlled chemical reaction, forced to send its electrons the long way round.',
why:'Batteries now gate electric transport and the whole renewable grid, and the trade-offs become obvious once you know the chemistry.',
body:['A battery has two electrodes in an electrolyte. One material wants to give up electrons, the other wants to take them. The electrolyte lets ions travel between them but blocks electrons — so the electrons must go through your circuit instead, doing work on the way. That deliberate detour is the entire idea.',
'In a lithium-ion cell nothing is consumed, only moved. Charging drives lithium ions into the graphite anode; discharging lets them travel back to the cathode. Because the materials are shuffled rather than used up, the cell is rechargeable — and because that shuffling slowly damages the structure, capacity fades over hundreds of cycles.'],
ex:['Cold slows the ion movement, which is why phone batteries appear to collapse in winter and recover indoors.',
'Fast charging generates heat and stresses the electrode structure, which is why it ages a battery faster.'],
facts:['Energy density is the hard constraint: petrol carries far more energy per kilogram than any current battery.',
'Storing a lithium cell at 100% charge accelerates degradation; around 50% is kindest for long storage.'],
keys:[['Anode/cathode','Where electrons leave and where they arrive.'],['Electrolyte','Conducts ions, blocks electrons — forcing the detour.'],['Cycle life','How many charge/discharge rounds before meaningful capacity loss.']],
sum:'Chemistry that wants to happen, allowed to proceed only if the electrons take a useful detour through your device.'},

{t:'How electricity works',c:'Physics',m:11,
hook:'Not electrons racing down a wire. A wave of pressure, moving at nearly light speed.',
why:'It powers everything, and the common mental model is wrong in a way that makes real circuits confusing.',
body:['Voltage is electrical pressure — a difference in potential between two points. Current is the rate of charge flow. Resistance opposes it. Ohm\'s law ties them together: V = I x R. That single relationship explains most everyday electrical behaviour.',
'Individual electrons drift astonishingly slowly, often less than a millimetre per second. What moves fast is the electromagnetic field that pushes them, which propagates near the speed of light. So a light comes on instantly not because electrons sprinted from the switch, but because the push arrived. Alternating current does not even move them net-forward; they jog back and forth 50 or 60 times a second.'],
ex:['Voltage does not hurt you, current does. High voltage matters because it drives current through your resistance.',
'Power is voltage times current, which is why transmission lines run at hundreds of thousands of volts: same power, far less current, far less heat lost.'],
facts:['Birds on a power line are safe because both feet are at the same potential — no difference, no current.',
'Household earth wires exist to give fault current a safer path than through you.'],
keys:[['Voltage','Potential difference — the push.'],['Current','Rate of charge flow — what actually does damage.'],['Resistance','Opposition to flow, converting energy to heat.']],
sum:'Voltage pushes, resistance resists, current results — and the push travels far faster than the charges do.'},

{t:'Why the sky is blue',c:'Physics',m:8,
hook:'Because small things scatter small waves much more strongly than large ones.',
why:'It is a one-sentence physical law you can then see operating every day, at sunset and in the sea.',
body:['Sunlight contains all visible wavelengths. Air molecules are far smaller than those wavelengths, and in that regime scattering strength rises with the inverse fourth power of wavelength — Rayleigh scattering. Blue light, with roughly half the wavelength of red, is scattered about sixteen times more strongly.',
'So blue light bounces around the atmosphere and reaches your eye from every direction, painting the whole sky. Looking at the Sun directly you see what is left after that removal, which is why it appears yellowish rather than white.'],
ex:['At sunset the light travels a much longer path through the atmosphere, so nearly all the blue is scattered away before reaching you, leaving red and orange.',
'Violet is scattered even more than blue, but the Sun emits less of it and our eyes are less sensitive to it.'],
facts:['On Mars the effect reverses at sunset: fine dust scatters differently, so Martian sunsets are blue.',
'From orbit, above the scattering layer, the sky is black even in full sunlight.'],
keys:[['Rayleigh scattering','Scattering by particles much smaller than the wavelength.'],['Wavelength','Shorter wavelengths scatter far more strongly.']],
sum:'Air scatters blue light in all directions far more than red, so the sky glows blue and the sunset burns red.'},

{t:'How stress affects the body',c:'Medicine',m:11,hook:'A brilliant short-term system that becomes corrosive when it never switches off.',why:'Chronic stress is implicated in most major diseases, and the mechanism is specific rather than vague.',body:['The acute response is two-stage. Over minutes, cortisol mobilises glucose and dampens systems you do not need right now — digestion, reproduction, and parts of immunity. This is superb for escaping a threat that ends.','The damage comes from the system not standing down. {t:'How the lungs exchange oxygen',c:'Medicine',m:10,hook:'Half a tennis court of surface, folded into your chest.',why:'It is a beautiful example of biology solving a surface-area problem, and it explains a lot about altitude and lung disease.',body:['Air travels down branching airways to about 300 million alveoli, tiny sacs with walls one cell thick. Capillaries press against them, so oxygen has only a fraction of a micrometre to cross. Gases move purely by diffusion down their concentration gradients — no pumping is involved at this stage.','Haemoglobin does the carrying. Each molecule binds four oxygens, and binding one makes the next easier — cooperativity, which produces an S-shaped curve. That shape means blood loads oxygen nearly fully in the lungs and unloads it readily in working tissue, where acidity and warmth shift the curve.'],ex:['Carbon monoxide binds haemoglobin about 200 times more tightly than oxygen, which is why it is lethal at low concentrations.','At altitude the air is the same 21% oxygen; it is the lower pressure that reduces how much crosses.'],facts:['Your urge to breathe is driven by rising carbon dioxide, not falling oxygen.','Emphysema destroys alveolar walls, merging many small sacs into fewer large ones and collapsing surface area.'],keys:[['Alveoli','Tiny sacs providing the exchange surface.'],['Diffusion','Passive movement down a concentration gradient.'],['Haemoglobin','The oxygen carrier, with cooperative binding.']],sum:'Enormous folded surface, a one-cell barrier, and a carrier molecule that grabs greedily and releases where needed.'},

{t:'How earthquakes happen',c:'Nature',m:10,hook:'Rock bends for centuries, then snaps in seconds.',why:'It explains why quakes cluster where they do and why prediction remains impossible while forecasting does not.',body:['Tectonic plates move a few centimetres a year, but faults do not slide smoothly — friction locks them. Strain accumulates in the surrounding rock, elastically, sometimes for centuries. When stress exceeds the fault\'s strength it slips suddenly, releasing the stored energy as seismic waves. This is elastic rebound.','Magnitude is logarithmic in amplitude but each whole step is about 32 times more energy, so a magnitude 7 releases roughly a thousand times the energy of a 5. Damage, though, depends as much on depth, distance and ground type: soft sediment amplifies shaking dramatically.'],ex:['P-waves arrive before the destructive S-waves, and early-warning systems buy seconds by detecting them first.','Aftershocks are the crust around the rupture readjusting, and follow a predictable decay.'],facts:['Most earthquakes occur at plate boundaries, but intraplate quakes happen and are poorly understood.','Buildings are usually what kills people, not the ground itself.'],keys:[['Fault','A fracture where blocks of crust move past each other.'],['Elastic rebound','Slow strain accumulation, sudden release.'],['Magnitude','A logarithmic measure of released energy.']],sum:'Slow strain, sudden slip, and shaking whose damage depends as much on soil and buildings as on size.'},

{t:'How volcanoes work',c:'Nature',m:10,hook:'Whether it oozes or explodes comes down to how sticky the magma is.',why:'One property — viscosity — explains the entire difference between Hawaii and Pompeii.',body:['Magma forms where rock partially melts: at subduction zones where water lowers the melting point, at spreading ridges where pressure drops, and over hot spots. Being less dense than surrounding rock, it rises and collects in chambers.','Eruption style is governed by silica content and dissolved gas. Low-silica magma is runny, so gas escapes easily and lava flows out — Hawaiian style. High-silica magma is stiff, gas cannot escape, pressure builds until the rock fails and the gas expands explosively, shredding the magma into ash. It is the same physics as opening a shaken bottle, with rock instead of glass.'],ex:['Mount St Helens in 1980 removed a bulging flank in a landslide, dropping pressure and triggering a lateral blast.','Pyroclastic flows — hot gas and ash moving at hundreds of km/h — are the main killer, not lava.'],facts:['Volcanic ash is pulverised rock, not soot; it melts in jet engines and abrades everything.','Large eruptions cool the planet for years by injecting sulphate aerosols into the stratosphere.'],keys:[['Viscosity','Stickiness of magma, which decides oozing versus exploding.'],['Silica content','The main driver of viscosity.'],['Pyroclastic flow','Fast, hot ash-and-gas avalanche.']],sum:'Runny magma leaks, stiff magma traps gas until the mountain fails — everything else follows from that.'},

{t:'Why the ocean is salty',c:'Nature',m:8,hook:'Rivers carry salt to the sea and only water leaves again.',why:'A neat example of an equilibrium you can reason out from first principles.',body:['Rain is slightly acidic and slowly dissolves minerals from rock. Rivers carry those dissolved ions to the ocean. Then evaporation removes water as vapour and leaves the ions behind. Over hundreds of millions of years the concentration built up to today\'s roughly 3.5%.','It is not still rising, because salt also leaves — precipitating into seafloor minerals, incorporated into hydrothermal reactions at ridges, and blown ashore as spray. {t:'How tides work',c:'Nature',m:10,hook:'Two bulges, and the Earth rotating through them.',why:'The two-bulge puzzle is a genuinely satisfying piece of reasoning that most explanations get wrong.',body:['The Moon pulls hardest on the near side of the Earth, less on the centre, least on the far side. What matters is the difference from the average. On the near side, water is pulled toward the Moon more than the Earth as a whole; on the far side, the Earth is pulled away from the water. Both produce a bulge, which is why there are two high tides a day rather than one.','The Sun contributes about 45% as much despite being far more massive, because tidal force falls off with the cube of distance. When Sun and Moon align you get spring tides; at right angles, weaker neap tides.'],ex:['The Bay of Fundy sees over 16 m of range because its shape resonates with the tidal period.','Tidal friction is slowing Earth\'s rotation and pushing the Moon away by about 3.8 cm per year.'],facts:['Tides lag the Moon\'s position because water has inertia and continents get in the way.','The solid Earth flexes too, by tens of centimetres.'],keys:[['Tidal force','The difference in gravity across a body, not gravity itself.'],['Spring/neap','Sun and Moon aligned or opposed.']],sum:'Gravity varies across the Earth; the difference raises two bulges and we rotate through both each day.'},

{t:'How seasons work',c:'Nature',m:8,hook:'Not distance from the Sun. {t:'What relativity actually means',c:'Physics',m:15,hook:'Everyone measures light at the same speed, and everything strange follows from that.',why:'It is the most misrepresented idea in physics, and the core of it needs no mathematics.',body:['Special relativity starts from two postulates: physics works the same in any non-accelerating frame, and light travels at the same speed for every observer regardless of their motion. The second is deeply counterintuitive and experimentally solid. If speed is fixed and speed is distance over time, then distance and time must be the things that change.','So moving clocks run slow and moving objects contract along their motion — not as illusions but as measurements that genuinely differ between observers. General relativity then adds gravity by reinterpreting it: mass and energy curve spacetime, and objects follow the straightest available path through that curved geometry. Falling is not being pulled, it is coasting.'],ex:['Muons created high in the atmosphere reach the ground despite decaying too fast to make the trip — from their frame the distance is contracted.','GPS satellites need both special and general corrections or positions drift kilometres a day.'],facts:['E = mc² says mass is a form of energy, which is why fusion and fission release so much from so little.','Nothing with mass reaches light speed, because the energy required rises without limit.'],keys:[['Invariant light speed','The postulate everything else follows from.'],['Time dilation','Moving clocks and clocks deeper in gravity run slow.'],['Spacetime curvature','Gravity as geometry rather than force.']],sum:'Hold the speed of light fixed for everyone and space and time have to bend to accommodate it.'},

{t:'How quantum mechanics works',c:'Physics',m:15,hook:'Not weird for the sake of it. Weird because reality at small scales is genuinely not made of little balls.',why:'It underpins chemistry, lasers, transistors and MRI, so it is not optional for understanding the modern world.',body:['At small scales, systems are described by a wavefunction giving the probability of each possible measurement outcome. Before measurement the system is genuinely in a superposition of possibilities — not secretly in one state we happen not to know. Interference experiments prove this: the possibilities interfere with each other, which mere ignorance could not do.','Measurement yields one definite outcome, apparently at random, with probabilities set by the wavefunction. Quantities like energy and angular momentum come in discrete steps rather than continuously, which is the "quantum". And entangled particles have correlated outcomes stronger than any pre-agreed plan could produce, which Bell tests have confirmed repeatedly.'],ex:['The double-slit experiment shows single particles interfering with themselves — until you detect which slit, and the pattern vanishes.','Transistors and flash memory rely on quantum tunnelling, particles crossing barriers they classically could not.'],facts:['Entanglement cannot send information faster than light; the outcomes are correlated but individually random.','The uncertainty principle is a property of waves, not a limit of our instruments.'],keys:[['Superposition','Being in multiple possible states in a way that interferes.'],['Wavefunction','What encodes the probabilities.'],['Entanglement','Correlations too strong for any pre-arranged explanation.']],sum:'Probabilities that interfere, quantities that come in steps, and correlations no classical story can reproduce.'},

{t:'How the universe expanded',c:'Space',m:13,hook:'Not an explosion into space. {t:'How money evolved',c:'Economics',m:11,hook:'Money is not a thing. It is a shared belief that keeps its promises.',why:'Understanding what money actually is makes inflation, banking and cryptocurrency far less mysterious.',body:['Barter has a coincidence problem: you must find someone who has what you want and wants what you have. Money solves it by being something everyone will accept, so trade splits into two halves. To work it must be durable, divisible, portable, and scarce enough that people trust it will hold value.','Commodity money — metals, salt, shells — had intrinsic value. Representative money was paper redeemable for metal. Today almost all money is fiat: it has value because a government declares it legal tender and, more importantly, because everyone else accepts it. Most of it is not printed at all but exists as entries in bank ledgers, created when banks make loans.'],ex:['The gold standard ended for good in 1971, so every major currency today is fiat.','Hyperinflation is the belief failing: once people stop expecting money to hold value, it stops holding value.'],facts:['Commercial banks create most new money by lending, not central banks by printing.','Yap islanders used enormous stone discs that never moved; ownership was simply remembered — a ledger, not a coin.'],keys:[['Medium of exchange','Solves the coincidence problem in barter.'],['Store of value','Holds purchasing power across time.'],['Fiat','Valuable by convention and law, not by substance.']],sum:'Money is a shared ledger of who is owed what, and its value rests entirely on the expectation that others will honour it.'},

{t:'How cryptocurrencies work',c:'Technology',m:14,hook:'A ledger everyone can check and nobody can quietly rewrite.',why:'Whatever you think of the price, the underlying problem it solves is genuinely interesting.',body:['Digital money has a double-spend problem: a file can be copied, so what stops you spending the same coin twice? Banks solve it with a trusted central ledger. Blockchains solve it without a trusted party: every participant holds the whole transaction history, and new blocks are chained together by cryptographic hashes so that altering an old one changes every hash after it.','Consensus decides which chain is real. Proof of work makes adding a block computationally expensive, so rewriting history means out-computing the entire honest network. Proof of stake instead requires validators to put capital at risk that can be destroyed if they cheat. Either way the security is economic: attacking costs more than it pays.'],ex:['Your wallet holds a private key, not coins. The coins are ledger entries; the key proves you may move them.','Smart contracts are programs stored on the chain that execute automatically, which is how most non-currency uses work.'],facts:['Transactions are pseudonymous, not anonymous: the entire history is public and often traceable.','Lose the private key and the funds are permanently unreachable — there is no recovery process by design.'],keys:[['Blockchain','A hash-chained ledger where changing the past invalidates everything after.'],['Consensus','How strangers agree which version is real.'],['Private key','Proof of the right to spend, and the only thing that matters.']],sum:'Replace a trusted bookkeeper with a public chained ledger and make rewriting it cost more than it could ever earn.'},

{t:'How businesses make money',c:'Business',m:11,hook:'Revenue is vanity, profit is sanity, cash is survival.',why:'Three numbers explain most of why companies succeed, fail and behave the way they do.',body:['A business creates something people value more than it costs to produce, and captures part of that gap. Revenue is what comes in; costs split into fixed, which do not vary with volume, and variable, which do. The gap between price and variable cost is contribution margin, and it must first cover fixed costs before anything is profit. That is the break-even point.','Cash flow is a separate matter, and it is what actually kills companies. A profitable business can die if customers pay in 90 days and suppliers demand payment in 30. Growing fast makes this worse, not better, because growth consumes cash before it produces it.'],ex:['Software has near-zero variable cost, so almost every extra sale is margin — which is why it attracts so much capital.','Supermarkets run tiny margins and survive on volume and fast inventory turnover.'],facts:['Most small business failures are cash flow, not lack of demand.','A price rise of a few percent, if volume holds, usually beats an equivalent cost cut.'],keys:[['Contribution margin','Price minus variable cost, per unit.'],['Fixed vs variable cost','What you pay anyway versus what scales with output.'],['Cash flow','Timing of money in and out — separate from profit.']],sum:'Create more value than you consume, cover your fixed costs, and never run out of cash while doing it.'},

{t:'How logical fallacies work',c:'Philosophy',m:10,hook:'Arguments that feel convincing precisely because they bypass the reasoning.',why:'Recognising a handful of patterns immunises you against most bad arguments you will encounter.',body:['A fallacy is a flaw in the structure of an argument, so the conclusion does not follow even if it happens to be true. Formal fallacies break the logic itself — affirming the consequent, for instance: "if it rained the ground is wet; the ground is wet; therefore it rained" ignores sprinklers.','Informal fallacies are more common and more persuasive because they exploit attention. Ad hominem attacks the person rather than the claim. Straw man rebuts a weaker version nobody argued. False dilemma offers two options when more exist. Appeal to authority substitutes status for evidence. Each is effective because it feels like an answer while never engaging the actual point.'],ex:['"You would say that, you work in the industry" may be relevant context but is not a refutation.','"Either we do this or we do nothing" is nearly always a false dilemma.'],facts:['Spotting a fallacy shows the argument fails, not that the conclusion is false — that is itself a fallacy, the fallacy fallacy.','Most fallacies work by shifting what is being evaluated without anyone noticing.'],keys:[['Ad hominem','Attacking the arguer instead of the argument.'],['Straw man','Refuting a distorted version of the claim.'],['False dilemma','Presenting fewer options than exist.']],sum:'Bad arguments usually change the subject convincingly; naming the move is most of the defence.'},

{t:'How critical thinking works',c:'Philosophy',m:11,hook:'Less about being sceptical of others, more about auditing yourself.',why:'It is the general-purpose skill underneath every other subject on this site.',body:['Critical thinking is separating claims from evidence and checking whether the evidence actually supports the claim. {t:'How algorithms work',c:'Programming',m:11,hook:'A recipe precise enough that a machine with no judgement can follow it.',why:'Algorithmic thinking is transferable far beyond programming, and complexity explains why some problems are hard forever.',body:['An algorithm is a finite sequence of unambiguous steps that turns an input into an output. An O(n²) approach that is fine for a hundred items is unusable for a million; an O(log n) approach barely notices the difference.','The classic strategies recur everywhere: divide and conquer splits a problem into smaller copies of itself; greedy algorithms take the locally best step and hope; dynamic programming stores sub-results so nothing is computed twice. Choosing the right shape matters far more than micro-optimising the wrong one.'],ex:['Binary search finds an item among a billion sorted entries in about 30 comparisons.','Sorting first often makes an apparently hard problem trivial afterwards.'],facts:['Some problems have no known efficient solution, and whether one exists is the P versus NP question.','Constant factors matter in practice: an O(n log n) algorithm can lose to O(n²) on small inputs.'],keys:[['Big-O','How cost grows with input size.'],['Divide and conquer','Break into smaller versions of the same problem.'],['Dynamic programming','Remember sub-answers instead of recomputing.']],sum:'Precision plus a sense of how cost scales — the second is what separates working from usable.'},

{t:'How machine learning differs from programming',c:'AI',m:10,hook:'Traditional code states the rules. {t:'How facial recognition works',c:'AI',m:10,hook:'Faces become points in a space where distance means similarity.',why:'It is deployed at borders, on phones and on streets, and knowing the mechanism clarifies both its power and its failure modes.',body:['A detector first finds face regions in the image. The network is trained so that two photos of the same person land close together and photos of different people land far apart, regardless of lighting or angle.','Recognition is then just distance. {t:'How 3D printing works',c:'Engineering',m:9,hook:'Manufacturing by addition rather than subtraction.',why:'It inverts a constraint that shaped industry for centuries: complexity now costs almost nothing.',body:['Conventional manufacturing removes material — cutting, drilling, milling — or forces it into a mould. A slicer converts a 3D model into instructions for each layer, and the machine deposits or fuses material accordingly.','The consequence is economic rather than merely technical. {t:'How engines work',c:'Engineering',m:11,hook:'Controlled explosions, four strokes, converting a bang into a turn.',why:'It is the machine that shaped the last century, and the cycle is easy to hold in your head.',body:['A four-stroke petrol engine repeats: intake draws in air and fuel; compression squeezes it, which raises its temperature and makes combustion violent; the power stroke ignites it and the expanding gas drives the piston down; exhaust pushes the spent gases out. {t:'How bridges stay up',c:'Architecture',m:10,hook:'Every bridge is a different answer to the same question: where does the load go?',why:'Once you can see the load path, bridges stop looking decorative and start looking inevitable.',body:['A beam bridge carries load by bending: the top compresses, the bottom stretches, and the material must resist both. That is why span is limited — bending stress grows fast with length. An arch converts load into compression along its curve and pushes outward at the ends, which is superb for stone and requires solid abutments.','A suspension bridge inverts the arch: cables hang in tension, the deck hangs from them, and the towers carry the load down. Steel is far stronger in tension than stone, which is why the longest spans are suspension bridges. Trusses work by triangulation — triangles cannot deform without changing member lengths, so a lattice of them is rigid at low weight.'],ex:['Roman arches survive two thousand years because stone is excellent in compression and the arch keeps it that way.','The Tacoma Narrows collapse in 1940 was aerodynamic flutter, not weight, and changed how decks are designed.'],facts:['Expansion joints exist because a long steel bridge changes length by a metre or more between summer and winter.','Cable-stayed bridges are cheaper than suspension for medium spans and now dominate that range.'],keys:[['Compression/tension','Being squeezed versus being pulled.'],['Load path','The route forces take from deck to ground.'],['Triangulation','Why trusses are rigid for their weight.']],sum:'Follow the load to the ground; the shape of the bridge is whatever makes that route efficient in the material available.'},

{t:'How skyscrapers are built',c:'Architecture',m:11,hook:'Above a certain height the enemy stops being gravity and becomes wind.',why:'It explains why tall buildings look the way they do, and the engineering constraints are counterintuitive.',body:['Early tall buildings used load-bearing walls, which had to get absurdly thick at the base. The steel frame changed everything: the skeleton carries the load and the exterior becomes a curtain wall, free to be glass. That single shift is what made height economical.','Past roughly forty storeys, wind dominates. A tower behaves like a vertical cantilever, and the problem is not collapse but sway and, crucially, acceleration that occupants can feel. Solutions include shaping the tower so it does not shed vortices rhythmically, stiffening it with a structural tube or outrigger system, and adding a tuned mass damper — a large weight that moves out of phase with the sway and cancels it.'],ex:['Taipei 101 has a 660-tonne pendulum damper, visible to visitors, that reduces sway by around 40%.','Setbacks and twists disrupt vortex shedding, which is why so many supertall towers taper or rotate.'],facts:['Lifts are a limiting factor: past a certain height, the shafts consume the floor area you were trying to gain.','Concrete cores are common because they handle both fire protection and lateral stiffness.'],keys:[['Curtain wall','A non-structural facade hung on the frame.'],['Vortex shedding','Rhythmic wind eddies that drive resonant sway.'],['Tuned mass damper','A counterweight that cancels motion.']],sum:'Steel frames solved gravity; everything above forty storeys is an argument with the wind.'},

{t:'How bees communicate',c:'Animals',m:9,hook:'A dance that encodes direction and distance, performed in the dark.',why:'It is one of the few known symbolic communication systems outside humans, and it was decoded by patient observation.',body:['A returning forager performs a waggle dance on the vertical comb. Other bees follow, feel the vibrations in the dark hive, and fly out on that heading.','The dance also compensates for time: bees adjust the angle as the Sun moves, so a dance performed an hour later still points correctly. {t:'How coral reefs work',c:'Nature',m:10,hook:'An animal, an alga and a limestone city they build together.',why:'Reefs support a quarter of marine species on a fraction of a percent of the seafloor, and are collapsing within our lifetimes.',body:['A coral is a colony of tiny animals that secrete a calcium carbonate skeleton. {t:'How glaciers shape landscapes',c:'Nature',m:9,hook:'Ice moves slowly enough to look still and hard enough to carve mountains in half.',why:'Much of the northern hemisphere\'s scenery is glacial, and reading it tells you what the ice did.',body:['Glaciers form where snow accumulates faster than it melts, compacting into ice that eventually flows under its own weight. They erode two ways: plucking, where meltwater freezes into cracks and rips blocks out as the ice moves on, and abrasion, where embedded rock grinds the bed like sandpaper.','The signature is shape. Rivers cut V-shaped valleys; glaciers, filling the whole valley, cut U-shaped ones. Cirques are the armchair hollows where ice originated. Fjords are glacial valleys drowned by rising sea level. Erratics — boulders of foreign rock — mark where ice carried and dropped them, which is how glacial extent was first mapped.'],ex:['Yosemite Valley\'s vertical walls and flat floor are textbook glacial U-shape.','Norway\'s fjords are hundreds of metres deeper than the sea outside them, because ice cut below sea level.'],facts:['Glaciers can move from centimetres to tens of metres a day, and surge unpredictably.','Ice cores from glaciers preserve atmospheric samples hundreds of thousands of years old.'],keys:[['Plucking','Freeze-thaw ripping rock from the bed.'],['Abrasion','Embedded debris grinding the bedrock.'],['U-shaped valley','The signature of glacial rather than river erosion.']],sum:'Ice flows, plucks and grinds — and leaves a valley shape rivers never make.'},

{t:'How weather forecasting works',c:'Science',m:11,hook:'Solve the physics of the atmosphere, badly, very fast, many times over.',why:'It is the most successful large-scale prediction system humans have built, and its limits are as instructive as its successes.',body:['Forecasting divides the atmosphere into a 3D grid and solves the equations of fluid motion, thermodynamics and moisture forward in time. Observations from satellites, balloons, aircraft and stations set the starting state — and the quality of that starting state matters enormously, because the atmosphere is chaotic: tiny initial errors grow exponentially.','That chaos is why forecasts use ensembles. The model is run many times from slightly different starting conditions; if the runs agree, confidence is high, and if they diverge, it is low. A "70% chance of rain" is literally the fraction of ensemble members that produced rain. It also sets a hard horizon — beyond roughly two weeks, useful deterministic forecasting is impossible in principle.'],ex:['Today\'s five-day forecast is about as accurate as the one-day forecast was in 1980.','Hurricane track forecasts have improved far faster than intensity forecasts, which depend on small-scale processes.'],facts:['Weather is chaotic; climate is a statistical average and is a genuinely different, more tractable problem.','Model grids are still coarse enough that thunderstorms must be approximated rather than simulated.'],keys:[['Initial conditions','The measured starting state, and the main error source.'],['Chaos','Exponential growth of small errors, capping predictability.'],['Ensemble','Many runs, whose spread expresses confidence.']],sum:'Physics on a grid, run many times because the atmosphere amplifies any error you started with.'},

{t:'How time zones work',c:'Geography',m:8,hook:'A political solution to an astronomical problem.',why:'It is a rare case where you can see the messy human layer sitting directly on top of clean physics.',body:['The Earth rotates 360 degrees in 24 hours, so 15 degrees of longitude corresponds to one hour of solar time. Timetables made that intolerable, so standard zones were adopted, anchored to the Greenwich meridian.','The physics would give 24 neat stripes. Politics does not. {t:'How maps represent the Earth',c:'Geography',m:10,hook:'Every flat map of a round planet is wrong. The only question is how.',why:'Map projections quietly shape how people picture the world, and the distortion is unavoidable rather than careless.',body:['A sphere cannot be flattened without distortion — this is a mathematical theorem, not a technical limitation. Every projection therefore chooses what to preserve: angles, areas, distances or directions, but never all at once.','Mercator preserves angles and shows any constant compass bearing as a straight line, which made it invaluable for navigation. The cost is area: it inflates landmasses toward the poles enormously, so Greenland appears comparable to Africa when Africa is roughly fourteen times larger. Equal-area projections fix that and distort shape instead.'],ex:['Web maps use Mercator largely because preserving angles keeps shapes locally correct when you zoom.','Great-circle flight paths look curved on a Mercator map because the shortest route on a sphere is not a straight line on that projection.'],facts:['The Peters projection was promoted politically as fairer to equatorial nations, and is genuinely equal-area but badly shape-distorted.','Globes are the only undistorted option, which is why they persist.'],keys:[['Projection','A rule for flattening a curved surface.'],['Conformal','Preserves angles and local shape.'],['Equal-area','Preserves relative size, at the cost of shape.']],sum:'Pick what you want to be true — angles or areas — because you cannot have both on a flat sheet.'},
