{t:'How reflexes work',c:'Biology',m:9,hook:'Your hand leaves the hot pan before your brain has been told there is a pan.',why:'It shows the nervous system is layered, with fast local circuits below conscious control.',body:['A reflex arc is deliberately short. A sensory neuron detects the stimulus and runs to the spinal cord, where it connects — sometimes directly, sometimes through a single interneuron — to a motor neuron that drives the muscle. The signal never goes to the brain before the action happens. That saves the tens of milliseconds a round trip would cost, which is the difference between a burn and a bad burn.','The brain is informed in parallel, which is why the pain arrives after the withdrawal. Reflexes also come with built-in coordination: the flexor withdrawal that pulls one leg up simultaneously stiffens the other so you do not fall over. {t:'How fingerprints form',c:'Biology',m:9,hook:'Identical twins share every gene and still have different fingerprints, because the pattern is partly an accident.',why:'It explains why fingerprints identify individuals at all, and what they are actually for.',body:['Ridges develop between about the tenth and sixteenth week of gestation, on pads of tissue on the fingertips. Genetics sets the broad type — arch, loop or whorl — along with pad size and timing. But the fine detail emerges from mechanical instability: the skin\'s basal layer grows faster than the layers around it and buckles, and exactly where each buckle initiates and how ridges meet and split depends on local conditions in the womb, including pressure, fluid, and the fetus\'s own position and movements.','That developmental noise is why the minutiae — the endings and bifurcations that identification actually uses — are effectively unique even between identical twins. {t:'Why humans have different eye colors',c:'Biology',m:10,hook:'There is no blue pigment in a blue eye. The colour is the same trick that makes the sky blue.',why:'It corrects the simple one-gene story taught in schools and explains why children surprise their parents.',body:['The iris contains one relevant pigment: melanin, which is brown. A lot of it in the front layer of the iris absorbs most light and the eye looks brown. Very little, and there is nothing to absorb the light, so it scatters within the cloudy iris tissue — scattering short wavelengths more strongly, exactly as the atmosphere does. The eye looks blue for the same structural reason the sky does, not because anything blue is present. Intermediate amounts, plus some yellowish pigment, produce green and hazel.','The genetics is not a single dominant-recessive pair. A regulatory region near the OCA2 gene, controlled by a nearby stretch called HERC2, accounts for most of the brown-blue variation, but at least a dozen other genes tune the result. So two blue-eyed parents can occasionally have a brown-eyed child, which the textbook model wrongly declares impossible. Everyone with blue eyes appears to share a single ancestral mutation that arose somewhere between six and ten thousand years ago.'],ex:['Many babies of European descent are born with blue eyes and darken over months as melanin is deposited.','Heterochromia — two different-coloured eyes — arises from uneven melanin distribution, and can be genetic or caused by injury.'],facts:['Blue eyes are more sensitive to bright light because less melanin means less absorption inside the eye.','Brown is by far the most common eye colour worldwide, at roughly 70 to 80% of people.'],keys:[['Melanin','The single pigment; quantity determines the outcome.'],['Structural colour','Blue produced by scattering, not by blue pigment.'],['Polygenic inheritance','Many genes, so simple dominance rules fail.']],sum:'Only brown pigment exists; blue eyes are blue because there is little of it and the tissue scatters light.'},
{t:'Why humans have different blood types',c:'Biology',m:11,hook:'Your blood type is a pattern of sugar molecules on your cells, and getting the wrong one is fatal within minutes.',why:'It explains transfusion rules, why they matter so much, and why the variation exists at all.',body:['Red blood cells carry surface markers built from sugars. The A allele adds one sugar, the B allele adds a different one, and the O allele adds nothing. Your immune system learns to tolerate whatever you have and produces antibodies against what you lack — someone with type A already carries anti-B antibodies without ever having encountered type B blood, probably because similar sugars are common on gut bacteria. That is why a mismatched transfusion causes immediate, catastrophic clumping.','Type O carries no A or B markers, so O negative can be given to almost anyone and is the universal donor; AB positive has all the markers and few antibodies, making it the universal recipient. The Rhesus factor is a separate protein, and it matters in pregnancy: an Rh-negative mother carrying an Rh-positive baby can be sensitised at birth and attack a subsequent Rh-positive pregnancy, which is why anti-D injections are given routinely. The variation likely persists because different types confer slightly different resistance to different pathogens.'],ex:['Cholera is somewhat more severe in people with type O, while type O appears to give some protection against severe malaria.','Blood typing takes minutes and is done before every transfusion, plus a cross-match against the actual donor unit.'],facts:['Type O is the most common worldwide, but frequencies vary sharply by population — type B is far more common across parts of Asia.','More than 30 blood group systems beyond ABO and Rh exist, and they matter for patients needing frequent transfusions.'],keys:[['Antigen','The surface marker that defines the type.'],['Pre-formed antibodies','Immunity against types you lack, present without prior exposure.'],['Rhesus factor','A separate protein, critical in pregnancy.']],sum:'Sugars on your red cells define your type, and your immune system attacks any pattern it did not grow up with.'},
{t:'How blood clotting works',c:'Medicine',m:12,hook:'A cascade of a dozen proteins amplifies a tiny signal into a plug, and every step exists to make it fast.',why:'It explains haemophilia, why aspirin thins blood, and why clots in the wrong place kill.',body:['Damage exposes collagen and tissue factor that are normally hidden inside the vessel wall. In parallel a cascade of clotting factors begins, each activated protein activating many copies of the next. {t:'How kidneys filter blood',c:'Medicine',m:12,hook:'Your kidneys filter 180 litres a day and give back 178 of them, which sounds absurd until you see why.',why:'The wasteful-looking design is what gives the body fine control over water, salt and acidity.',body:['Each kidney holds about a million nephrons. Blood enters a tuft of capillaries under pressure, and that pressure forces water and small molecules through a filter into a tubule — indiscriminately. Everything small enough goes: water, salts, glucose, amino acids, urea. Cells and large proteins stay behind. This crude first pass produces around 180 litres of filtrate a day, which is roughly your entire blood plasma volume filtered every half hour.','The tubule then reclaims almost all of it, selectively. Glucose and amino acids are pumped back completely, most sodium and water follow, and a long hairpin loop sets up a salt gradient in the surrounding tissue that lets the kidney concentrate urine far beyond blood. The advantage of filtering everything then choosing what to take back is control: hormones can adjust reabsorption minute by minute, so the same organ handles dehydration, salt loading and acid-base balance without redesign.'],ex:['Antidiuretic hormone makes the final tubule permeable to water, which is why dehydration produces small volumes of dark urine.','Diabetes gets its name from glucose exceeding the reabsorption capacity and spilling into urine, dragging water with it.'],facts:['Kidneys also make erythropoietin, which tells the marrow to produce red cells — kidney failure causes anaemia for this reason.','You can live normally with one kidney, since filtration capacity is built with a large reserve.'],keys:[['Glomerular filtration','Pressure-driven, indiscriminate first pass.'],['Selective reabsorption','Taking back what the body needs, under hormonal control.'],['Countercurrent multiplier','The loop that builds a gradient for concentrating urine.']],sum:'Filter everything indiscriminately, then reclaim what you need — because choosing what to keep is easier to regulate.'},
{t:'How the liver processes toxins',c:'Medicine',m:12,hook:'The liver does not remove poisons so much as chemically rewrite them until the kidneys can flush them out.',why:'It explains drug interactions, why alcohol damages the liver, and why grapefruit warnings exist.',body:['Most harmful substances are fat-soluble, which is precisely why they accumulate in the body and cross into cells: the kidneys can only excrete water-soluble things. The liver\'s job is conversion. In phase one, a family of enzymes called cytochrome P450 adds or exposes a reactive chemical group, usually by oxidation. In phase two, the modified molecule is conjugated — a large water-soluble group is bolted on — making it too soluble to re-enter cells and easy to excrete in urine or bile.','The system has a dangerous quirk: phase one often makes things more toxic before phase two makes them safe. Paracetamol is harmless until P450 converts a fraction of it into a highly reactive metabolite, which is normally neutralised immediately by glutathione. Exceed the dose and glutathione runs out, the metabolite attacks liver cells directly, and the damage may not become obvious for a day. Drug interactions work the same way — one substance inducing or blocking a P450 enzyme changes how fast another is cleared.'],ex:['Grapefruit inhibits a specific P450 enzyme in the gut, so affected drugs are absorbed far more completely and can reach dangerous levels.','Alcohol is processed to acetaldehyde first, which is more toxic than alcohol and causes much of a hangover.'],facts:['The liver can regenerate substantially — a large fraction can be removed and will regrow, which makes living donation possible.','Cirrhosis is irreversible because regeneration is replaced by scar tissue that cannot perform the chemistry.'],keys:[['Cytochrome P450','Enzymes that chemically modify foreign substances.'],['Conjugation','Attaching a water-soluble group for excretion.'],['Toxic intermediate','Phase one sometimes producing something worse than the original.']],sum:'The liver makes fat-soluble poisons water-soluble so the kidneys can flush them, occasionally creating something worse en route.'},
{t:'How bones repair themselves',c:'Medicine',m:11,hook:'A healed break is often stronger than the bone was before, and the process rebuilds rather than patches.',why:'Bone is the only tissue that heals by regenerating itself completely rather than scarring.',body:['A fracture first bleeds, forming a clot around the break that becomes a scaffold within hours. Inflammatory cells clear debris and dead bone, and release signals recruiting stem cells from the surrounding tissue and marrow. Over the following weeks those cells lay down a soft callus of cartilage and fibrous tissue that bridges the gap and stabilises it crudely — enough to stop movement, not enough to bear load.','The soft callus is then mineralised into a hard callus of woven bone: mechanically adequate but disorganised. The final stage is the interesting one. Over months to years the body remodels it, with osteoclasts removing bone and osteoblasts depositing new bone along the lines of mechanical stress. {t:'How joints work',c:'Biology',m:10,hook:'The cartilage in your knee has a coefficient of friction lower than ice on ice, and it cannot repair itself.',why:'It explains arthritis, why joint damage is permanent, and what replacement surgery is actually replacing.',body:['A synovial joint is a gap between two bones, each capped with articular cartilage, enclosed in a capsule filled with synovial fluid. The cartilage is a sponge of collagen and water; loading it squeezes fluid out onto the surface, and unloading draws it back — a self-pressurising lubrication system so effective that the friction is several times lower than ice sliding on ice. Ligaments constrain which motions are possible, and tendons transmit muscle force across the joint.','The fatal flaw is that articular cartilage has no blood supply. Joint replacement substitutes metal and polyethylene surfaces for the worn ones, which works well but has a finite lifespan, which is why surgeons delay it in younger patients.'],ex:['The knee\'s menisci are separate wedges of fibrocartilage that spread load; their outer third has blood supply and can heal, the inner two-thirds cannot.','The popping sound when a joint cracks is a gas bubble forming in the synovial fluid, not bones or cartilage.'],facts:['Cartilage is about 80% water, and its stiffness comes from that water being trapped in a collagen mesh under pressure.','Weight loss reduces knee load by several times the mass lost, because of the leverage involved in walking.'],keys:[['Articular cartilage','Low-friction cap with no blood supply, so no real repair.'],['Synovial fluid','Lubricant and nutrient source, circulated by movement.'],['Osteoarthritis','Cartilage loss leading to painful bone-on-bone contact.']],sum:'A pressurised fluid film makes joints almost frictionless, and the cartilage that enables it cannot heal once worn.'},
{t:'How tendons work',c:'Biology',m:10,hook:'Tendons are springs. When your foot lands, the Achilles stretches and stores energy like a spring; on push-off it recoils and gives most of it back, cutting the metabolic cost of running substantially. {t:'Why wounds heal',c:'Medicine',m:11,hook:'Healing is four overlapping programmes that must run in the right order, and most chronic wounds are stuck in one.',why:'It explains scars, why some wounds never close, and why keeping a wound moist beats letting it scab.',body:['First comes haemostasis: vessels constrict and a clot seals the breach within minutes. {t:'How fat is stored in the body',c:'Medicine',m:11,hook:'Fat cells rarely disappear. {t:'How the body uses carbohydrates',c:'Medicine',m:11,hook:'Every carbohydrate becomes glucose. {t:'How confirmation bias works',c:'Psychology',m:11,hook:'You do not search for the truth. You search for reasons to keep believing what you already believe.',why:'It is the single most consequential thinking error, and it operates invisibly by design.',body:['Confirmation bias operates at three stages. You seek information more likely to support your view than to challenge it. You interpret ambiguous evidence as favourable. And you remember supporting evidence better than contradicting evidence. None of this feels like bias from the inside — it feels like being right, because at every step the process was subjectively reasonable.','What makes it stubborn is that it is not stupidity. Intelligent, numerate people show it strongly, and sometimes more strongly, because they are better at generating arguments for whatever they already think. {t:'How the Dunning-Kruger effect works',c:'Psychology',m:11,hook:'The skills needed to be good at something are the same skills needed to recognise you are bad at it.',why:'It is constantly misquoted, and the real finding is subtler and more useful than the meme.',body:['Dunning and Kruger found that poor performers overestimated their ability substantially, while top performers slightly underestimated theirs. Someone with no grasp of grammar cannot spot their own grammatical errors, and therefore cannot know they are making them.','The popular version — that idiots think they are geniuses — is wrong. {t:'How peer pressure works',c:'Psychology',m:11,hook:'Most peer pressure involves nobody applying any pressure at all.',why:'The passive version is far more powerful than the confrontational version everyone is warned about.',body:['Classic experiments separate two forces. {t:'How cognitive biases affect decisions',c:'Psychology',m:12,hook:'Biases are not random errors. They are systematic, predictable, and they survive knowing about them.',why:'Knowing the catalogue is less useful than knowing which situations produce them and how to change those.',body:['The brain runs two broad modes: fast, automatic judgement and slow, effortful reasoning. The fast system uses heuristics — shortcuts that are efficient and usually right. Biases are the predictable places where those shortcuts fail. Availability makes vividly memorable events feel more probable, so people fear plane crashes over car journeys. Anchoring drags estimates toward whatever number was mentioned first, even an irrelevant one. Loss aversion makes a loss hurt roughly twice as much as an equivalent gain feels good, which distorts every risky choice.','The uncomfortable finding is that education barely helps. Experts show the same biases within their expertise, and people warned about anchoring still anchor. What does work is structural: decide criteria before seeing options, use base rates explicitly, take an outside view by asking how similar projects actually turned out, and get independent judgements before discussion rather than after. {t:'How trust develops',c:'Psychology',m:11,hook:'Trust is built slowly by small consistent acts and destroyed quickly by a single inconsistent one, and the asymmetry is rational.',why:'It underlies every relationship, contract and institution, and the asymmetry explains why repair is so hard.',body:['Trust is a judgement combining two things: competence, whether someone can deliver, and benevolence, whether they intend to. Because trust is essentially a prediction about future behaviour, the evidence has to accumulate, and one instance proves very little. A trustworthy person should almost never betray you, so a single betrayal is highly informative and heavily updates the estimate, while any individual honest act is weakly informative because untrustworthy people behave well most of the time too. {t:'How stereotypes form',c:'Psychology',m:12,hook:'Stereotyping is category learning applied to people, using the same machinery that makes you good at everything else.',why:'Understanding it as a cognitive process rather than a moral failing points at what actually reduces it.',body:['The brain categorises constantly, because treating every object as unique would be paralysing. Categories let you generalise from limited experience, and they work well for objects. Applied to people, the same process produces stereotypes: a generalisation about a group applied to an individual. It is efficient, it is often partly grounded in some real correlation, and it is systematically wrong at the individual level because within-group variation almost always dwarfs between-group differences.','Several mechanisms then entrench them. {t:'How Bluetooth works',c:'Technology',m:11,hook:'Bluetooth survives a crowded radio band by hopping frequencies 1600 times a second.',why:'It connects everything you own wirelessly, and its design choices explain every quirk you have cursed at.',body:['Bluetooth operates in the 2.4 GHz band, which is unlicensed and therefore shared with Wi-Fi, microwave ovens and baby monitors. Its answer to that chaos is frequency hopping: the band is split into 79 narrow channels and the connected devices hop between them in a pseudo-random sequence, synchronised to a shared clock, 1600 times a second. If one channel is being jammed at a given instant, only a tiny fragment of data is lost and is retransmitted on the next hop.','Pairing exchanges keys so devices recognise each other later and encrypt traffic. The other decisive design choice is power. Bluetooth Low Energy, introduced in version 4.0, keeps devices asleep almost all the time and wakes them only to send tiny bursts, which is how a fitness tracker or a beacon runs for a year on a coin cell. The tradeoff is bandwidth: classic Bluetooth handles audio streams, while BLE handles small packets of sensor data and cannot carry high-quality audio.'],ex:['Audio delay in wireless headphones comes from buffering and codec processing, not from the radio itself — which is why gaming modes cut quality to reduce latency.','Adaptive frequency hopping detects channels Wi-Fi is using and stops hopping onto them, which is why the two coexist better than they used to.'],facts:['The name comes from a tenth-century Danish king, and the logo is his initials in runes.','Range depends on power class: most phones are class 2 at about 10 metres, while industrial class 1 devices reach 100.'],keys:[['Frequency hopping','Rapid channel switching to survive interference.'],['Pairing','Key exchange establishing a trusted, encrypted link.'],['Low Energy','Sleeping between tiny bursts to run for years on a coin cell.']],sum:'Hop constantly across a noisy band, sleep between bursts, and exchange keys once so the pair recognise each other.'},
{t:'How NFC works',c:'Technology',m:10,hook:'A contactless card has no battery. The reader powers it with a magnetic field from a few centimetres away.',why:'It is behind every tap-to-pay, transit card and hotel key, and the short range is a security feature.',body:['NFC works by inductive coupling at 13.56 MHz. The reader runs alternating current through a coil, generating an oscillating magnetic field. Bring a second coil into that field and current is induced in it — the same principle as a transformer, or a wireless charger, but at low power. That induced current powers the chip in the card, which then modulates the load on its own coil, and the reader detects the resulting change in its field. The card talks back by varying how much energy it absorbs.','Range is deliberately tiny — a few centimetres — because the field strength falls off very steeply with distance. That is a security property, not a limitation: an attacker cannot silently read your card across a room. Payments add cryptography on top. The chip holds a secret key it never transmits, and for each transaction it produces a one-time cryptogram signed with that key, so intercepting one transaction gives an attacker nothing reusable.'],ex:['Hotel key cards and transit passes are usually passive tags with no power source at all, activated only in the reader\'s field.','Phone payments substitute a device-specific token for your real card number, so the merchant never sees the actual card.'],facts:['NFC evolved from RFID, and the main difference is that NFC devices can act as both reader and tag.','Contactless limits exist to cap losses on a lost card, not because the cryptography is weaker for larger amounts.'],keys:[['Inductive coupling','Powering a passive chip by magnetic field.'],['Load modulation','The card replying by varying the energy it draws.'],['Tokenisation','Replacing the real card number with a single-use substitute.']],sum:'The reader powers the card by induction, the card answers by absorbing energy in patterns, and range is short on purpose.'},
{t:'How QR codes work',c:'Technology',m:10,hook:'Those three big squares in the corners exist so a camera can work out which way up the code is.',why:'The design is a masterclass in engineering for the real world, where codes are creased, dirty and photographed at an angle.',body:['A QR code is a grid of black and white modules encoding binary data. The three large squares are finder patterns, placed at three of four corners: the camera locates them first, and their arrangement immediately gives orientation and perspective — with three known points you can undo the rotation and skew of a photograph taken at an angle. Smaller alignment patterns correct for curvature on bent surfaces, and timing patterns of alternating modules let the scanner determine the exact grid size.','The critical feature is Reed-Solomon error correction, the same family of codes used on CDs and in deep-space transmission. Depending on the level chosen, up to 30% of the code can be destroyed and the data still recovers perfectly. That is why codes work with a logo pasted over the middle, and why a scuffed sticker still scans. Data is also masked with a pattern chosen to avoid large blank regions, since even distribution of dark and light modules makes decoding more reliable.'],ex:['Codes with a company logo in the centre work because the covered area falls within the error correction budget.','A QR code containing just a short URL uses few modules and produces a sparse, easily scanned pattern.'],facts:['They were invented in 1994 by Denso Wave to track car parts, and the patent was deliberately never enforced.','The highest correction level trades capacity for durability, storing less data in the same size code.'],keys:[['Finder patterns','Three corner squares giving orientation and perspective.'],['Reed-Solomon coding','Error correction recovering data from a damaged code.'],['Masking','Rearranging modules to avoid hard-to-read blank areas.']],sum:'Corner squares fix the geometry and heavy error correction means a third of the code can be destroyed and still read.'},

{t:'How touchscreens detect fingers',c:'Technology',m:10,hook:'Your phone screen is not detecting pressure. It is detecting that your finger is electrically leaky.',why:'It explains gloves, wet screens, styluses and why old cash machines needed a firm press.',body:['Modern phones use projected capacitive sensing. A transparent grid of conductors is layered under the glass, driven with a small alternating voltage that creates tiny electric fields at every intersection. The human body is conductive and capacitively coupled to ground, so a fingertip near an intersection draws some of that field away, measurably changing the capacitance there. The controller scans the whole grid many times a second and reports the coordinates of every change, which is how multi-touch works — each finger perturbs its own region.','Resistive touchscreens, still found on older kiosks and industrial equipment, work completely differently: two flexible conductive layers separated by a gap, pressed into contact by force. That responds to anything — a fingernail, a pen, a gloved hand — but supports only one touch point and needs actual pressure. Capacitive screens fail with ordinary gloves because the insulating fabric blocks the coupling, and misbehave when wet because water is also conductive and creates false touches.'],ex:['Touchscreen gloves have conductive thread woven into the fingertips to restore the electrical path.','A capacitive stylus is simply a conductive tip large enough to register, which is why cheap ones are so blunt.'],facts:['Force Touch and Haptic Touch use separate pressure sensors or timing, because capacitance alone cannot measure how hard you press.','Screens report touches at 120 Hz or higher, well above the display refresh rate, to keep dragging feeling attached to your finger.'],keys:[['Projected capacitance','Sensing a finger by the field it draws away.'],['Multi-touch','Independent detection at each grid intersection.'],['Resistive touch','Pressure-based, works with anything but only one point.']],sum:'A grid of electric fields under the glass notices where your conductive finger steals some of the charge.'},
{t:'How smartphone cameras work',c:'Technology',m:12,hook:'A phone photo is not captured. A tiny sensor collects little light, so physics guarantees more noise and less control over depth of field than a large camera. Phones compensate with computation. Pressing the shutter does not take one exposure; the camera has already been capturing a rolling buffer of frames, and it selects and aligns several, then merges them. Averaging aligned frames cancels random noise, and combining differently exposed frames recovers both highlight and shadow detail — that is HDR, done automatically on every shot.','On top of that sits a stack of processing: demosaicing to reconstruct colour from a filtered sensor, lens correction, tone mapping, sharpening, and machine-learning models trained to recognise scene content and process faces, skies and food differently. Portrait mode estimates a depth map, from a second lens or from a neural network, and blurs the background artificially. The result is that two phones photographing the same scene produce visibly different images — the differences are largely software opinion, not optics.'],ex:['Night mode holds the shutter open across many frames and aligns them, which is why you must hold still for a second or two.','Portrait mode errors on wispy hair and glasses reveal that the blur is computed from an imperfect depth estimate.'],facts:['Phones capture frames continuously before you press the button, which is how zero shutter lag works.','Multiple rear lenses are usually separate fixed focal lengths, and zooming between them switches cameras and crops.'],keys:[['Computational photography','Building one image from many frames.'],['HDR merging','Combining exposures to capture both highlights and shadows.'],['Semantic processing','Machine learning applying different treatment by scene content.']],sum:'A small sensor is rescued by merging many frames and applying heavy learned processing to the result.'},
{t:'How image compression works',c:'Technology',m:12,hook:'JPEG works by throwing away detail your eye was never going to notice, in a very specific order.',why:'It explains file size choices, why some images look blocky, and why PNG exists alongside JPEG.',body:['JPEG starts by exploiting a fact about human vision: we are far more sensitive to brightness than to colour detail. So the image is converted from red-green-blue into brightness plus two colour channels, and the colour channels are downsampled — often to a quarter of the resolution — which loses almost nothing perceptually. The image is then split into 8×8 blocks and each block is transformed into frequency components by a discrete cosine transform: a description of the block as a sum of smooth and fine patterns.','The compression happens next. High-frequency components, which encode fine detail, are divided by larger numbers and rounded — quantisation — and many round to zero. The quality setting is just how aggressive that division is. The mostly-zero result is then packed losslessly. Because each 8×8 block is quantised independently, pushing quality low makes block boundaries visible, which is the characteristic JPEG artefact. PNG takes a different approach entirely: fully lossless, using prediction and general-purpose compression, which is why it is better for screenshots and diagrams but far larger for photographs.'],ex:['Repeatedly saving a JPEG degrades it, because each save re-quantises the already-quantised data.','Sharp text and hard edges compress badly in JPEG, since edges are exactly the high-frequency detail being discarded.'],facts:['Colour subsampling alone typically halves the data with essentially no visible change.','WebP and AVIF achieve substantially smaller files than JPEG at the same quality by using more sophisticated prediction and transforms.'],keys:[['Chroma subsampling','Storing colour at lower resolution than brightness.'],['Discrete cosine transform','Rewriting a block as frequency components.'],['Quantisation','Rounding away high frequencies, which is the lossy step.']],sum:'Convert to frequencies, round away the fine detail your eye barely uses, then pack what remains.'},
{t:'How video compression works',c:'Technology',m:12,hook:'Most frames of a video are not stored at all. Only the differences from the previous frame are.',why:'It is why streaming is possible at all, and why fast motion looks worse than a static scene.',body:['Video is enormously redundant: consecutive frames are nearly identical. Codecs exploit this with three frame types. An I-frame is a complete image, compressed much like a JPEG. A P-frame stores only what changed from the previous frame. A B-frame is more efficient still, predicted from both earlier and later frames. A keyframe interval determines how often a full I-frame appears, which is why seeking jumps to the nearest one rather than the exact position you clicked.','The real work is motion compensation. Rather than storing pixel differences, the encoder divides the frame into blocks and searches the previous frame for where each block moved to, storing a motion vector plus a small residual for whatever the prediction got wrong. A panning shot of an unchanging scene therefore compresses to almost nothing — everything simply moved. Fast, chaotic motion defeats prediction, the residuals grow, and at a fixed bitrate the encoder must degrade quality, which is why confetti, water and rapid camera movement look worst.'],ex:['A talking-head video compresses extremely well because only the face moves against a static background.','Scene changes force a new I-frame, which is why a bitrate spike often accompanies a cut.'],facts:['Modern codecs like AV1 and HEVC achieve roughly half the bitrate of H.264 at similar quality, at much higher encoding cost.','Streaming services encode many versions at different bitrates and switch between them mid-playback as your bandwidth changes.'],keys:[['I, P and B frames','Complete, forward-predicted and bidirectionally predicted frames.'],['Motion compensation','Storing where a block moved instead of its pixels.'],['Residual','The small correction for whatever prediction got wrong.']],sum:'Store one full frame occasionally, then describe everything after it as motion plus small corrections.'},
{t:'How streaming services work',c:'Technology',m:12,hook:'The video you are watching probably came from a server in your own city, not from the company that made it.',why:'It explains buffering, why quality shifts mid-scene, and why streaming survives peak-time demand at all.',body:['Streaming does not send one file. Each title is encoded into several quality levels and each level is cut into segments of a few seconds. Your player downloads a manifest listing what is available, then requests segments one at a time, measuring how fast each arrives. If throughput drops it requests the next segment at a lower bitrate; if it rises it steps up. That is adaptive bitrate streaming, and it is why quality changes without playback stopping — buffering only occurs when the buffer empties faster than segments arrive even at the lowest quality.','The second half is distribution. Serving millions of simultaneous streams from central data centres would be impossible, so providers push copies of popular content to caches inside internet providers\' own networks, sometimes on hardware physically installed there. Your request is routed to the nearest copy, which shortens the path, reduces cost and removes most of the load from the backbone. {t:'How push notifications work',c:'Technology',m:10,hook:'Your phone is not checking for messages. Instead the operating system maintains one persistent connection to a central push service — Apple\'s APNs or Google\'s FCM. An app\'s server sends the message to that service, which routes it down the single open connection to the correct device, and the OS then wakes the relevant app. One connection serves every app on the phone.','That connection is kept alive with periodic tiny heartbeat packets, timed to coincide with other radio activity where possible so the modem does not wake up repeatedly. It is also why the OS controls delivery entirely: it can batch notifications, delay low-priority ones, and refuse to wake apps that are misbehaving. Notifications carry only a small payload, so tapping one typically triggers the app to fetch the actual content — which is why a notification can appear for a message that then takes a moment to load.'],ex:['Aggressive battery optimisation on some Android phones delays or drops notifications, because the OS is suppressing wakeups.','Notifications still arrive when an app is fully closed, because the OS connection, not the app, is what receives them.'],facts:['Push payloads are strictly size-limited, which is why rich content is fetched after the tap.','Providers can mark notifications as high priority to bypass batching, and abuse of that flag is policed by both platforms.'],keys:[['Persistent connection','One always-open channel shared by every app.'],['Push service','A central relay routing messages to the right device.'],['Heartbeat','Small keepalive packets, aligned to save radio wakeups.']],sum:'One connection stays open for the whole phone, and a central service pushes messages down it to the right app.'},
{t:'How email delivery works',c:'Technology',m:12,hook:'Email was designed with no security at all, and everything protecting it today was bolted on afterwards.',why:'It explains spam, spoofing, and why perfectly legitimate messages land in junk folders.',body:['Sending an email means your client hands the message to a mail server by SMTP. That server looks up the recipient domain\'s MX records in DNS to find which server accepts its mail, connects, and delivers. The receiving server stores it, and the recipient collects it later by IMAP. The protocol is old and trusting: nothing in original SMTP verifies that the sender address is genuine, which is why spoofing was trivial and spam became overwhelming.','Three layers were added to fix it, and all three are DNS records the domain owner publishes. SPF lists which servers are permitted to send mail for the domain. DKIM cryptographically signs outgoing messages so the receiver can verify they were not altered and did come from the domain. DMARC ties the two together and tells receivers what to do when checks fail. Receiving servers combine these with reputation scoring — sending history, complaint rates, content signals — to decide between inbox, spam folder and outright rejection. A legitimate message from a misconfigured domain fails these checks and is filtered, which is the usual reason mail mysteriously vanishes.'],ex:['A newsletter sent through a third-party service needs that service authorised in your SPF record, or it will be treated as forged.','Sudden large sending volume from a new address triggers reputation filters regardless of content.'],facts:['Estimates put well over half of all email traffic as spam, filtered before most users ever see it.','Email content is not end-to-end encrypted by default; transport encryption protects it between servers but each server can read it.'],keys:[['SMTP and MX records','Delivery protocol and the DNS lookup that finds the destination.'],['SPF and DKIM','Authorising senders and signing messages cryptographically.'],['Reputation','Sending history and complaint rates deciding inbox placement.']],sum:'An old trusting protocol delivers the mail, and DNS-published authentication plus reputation decide whether it is believed.'},
{t:'How domain names work',c:'Technology',m:11,hook:'Every address you type is translated by a distributed database that nobody owns and everybody depends on.',why:'DNS is the internet\'s phone book, and it is the single most common cause of things being mysteriously down.',body:['Computers route by IP address; humans use names. DNS bridges the two through a hierarchy read right to left. Your resolver asks a root server which servers handle .com, asks those which servers handle example.com, and asks those for the address of www.example.com. Each answer comes with a time-to-live, and results are cached at every level — which is why the system handles enormous query volumes and why changes take time to propagate.','Registration is separate from resolution. Registries operate each top-level domain, registrars sell names to the public, and owning a name means holding an entry pointing at your chosen nameservers. Those nameservers hold the actual records: A records for addresses, MX for mail, CNAME for aliases, TXT for verification and email authentication. {t:'How cookies work',c:'Technology',m:11,hook:'HTTP has no memory. A cookie solves it. The server sends a small named value with its response, the browser stores it, and it is attached to every subsequent request to that domain. That is how a login persists — the cookie carries a session identifier the server can look up. Attributes control the behaviour: expiry, which paths receive it, Secure to require HTTPS, and HttpOnly to hide it from JavaScript so a script injection cannot steal a session.','The privacy question turns on first versus third party. A cookie from the site you are visiting is first-party and mostly functional. A cookie set by an embedded resource — an ad, a tracking pixel, a social button — belongs to that third party, and because the same third party is embedded across thousands of sites, it sees a cookie it recognises on each one and can assemble a browsing profile. Browsers have largely blocked third-party cookies now, which has pushed tracking toward fingerprinting and server-side identifiers instead.'],ex:['A shopping cart that survives a browser restart is a persistent cookie; one that empties is a session cookie.','SameSite attributes stop cookies being sent on cross-site requests, which blocks a whole class of forgery attacks.'],facts:['Cookies are capped at about 4 KB each, which is why they hold identifiers rather than actual data.','Consent banners exist because of laws requiring permission for non-essential cookies, not because cookies became more invasive.'],keys:[['Statelessness','HTTP forgetting everything between requests.'],['Session identifier','A token linking requests to a logged-in account.'],['Third-party cookie','Set by an embedded resource, enabling cross-site tracking.']],sum:'A small value handed back on every request gives a stateless protocol memory — and gives embedded third parties a way to follow you.'},

{t:'How firewalls work',c:'Cybersecurity',m:11,hook:'A firewall is a list of rules about which conversations are allowed, and the interesting part is that it remembers them.',why:'It is the most basic network defence and the one most often assumed to do more than it does.',body:['The simplest firewall filters packets on their headers: source and destination address, port and protocol, allow or deny. That alone is weak, because it treats every packet independently and cannot tell a reply from an unsolicited probe. Stateful firewalls fixed this by tracking connections. When you initiate an outbound connection, the firewall records it and automatically permits the matching return traffic, while unsolicited inbound packets that match no known conversation are dropped. That single idea is why a home router protects you by default.','Application-layer firewalls go further and inspect content, not just headers — recognising that traffic on port 443 is a specific protocol, blocking known attack patterns, or enforcing rules about what may be uploaded. What no firewall does is protect against traffic you invited. A user downloading malware over an allowed HTTPS connection, or a phishing page collecting a password, passes through a correctly configured firewall without triggering anything, which is why perimeter defence alone stopped being sufficient long ago.'],ex:['Your home router runs a stateful firewall implicitly through NAT — inbound connections have no internal address to map to and are simply dropped.','Egress filtering restricts what may leave the network, which is often what detects a compromised machine phoning home.'],facts:['Default-deny — block everything, then permit specific things — is far safer than default-allow, and is standard practice.','Most modern breaches begin with allowed traffic, which is why zero-trust architectures assume the perimeter has already failed.'],keys:[['Packet filtering','Allow or deny based on header fields alone.'],['Stateful inspection','Tracking connections so replies are recognised.'],['Default deny','Blocking everything except what is explicitly permitted.']],sum:'Rules decide which conversations may start, connection tracking allows the replies, and anything you invited gets through.'},
{t:'How antivirus software works',c:'Cybersecurity',m:11,hook:'Signature matching stopped being enough decades ago, because malware now rewrites itself on every copy.',why:'It explains false positives, why detection is never complete, and what actually catches modern attacks.',body:['The original approach was signatures: hashes or byte patterns unique to known malicious files, checked against a database updated constantly. It is fast and produces almost no false positives, and it is useless against anything new or altered. Polymorphic malware encrypts or rewrites itself with each infection, so no two copies share a signature, which forced the industry to change approach.','Modern products layer several methods. Heuristics look for suspicious structural features rather than exact matches. Behavioural monitoring watches what a program does at runtime — hooking system calls, encrypting many files quickly, injecting into other processes — and intervenes on the pattern rather than the file. Sandboxing runs suspicious files in an isolated environment first. Machine learning classifiers score files on thousands of features. Each layer trades precision for coverage, which is the fundamental tension: more aggressive detection catches more real threats and also flags more legitimate software.'],ex:['Ransomware is often caught behaviourally, by the distinctive pattern of rapidly encrypting many files, rather than by any signature.','Developer tools and installers are frequent false positives because they legitimately do things malware also does.'],facts:['Detection rates against genuinely novel malware are far below the near-perfect numbers quoted for known samples.','Keeping software patched prevents more compromises in practice than any scanner, because most attacks use known vulnerabilities.'],keys:[['Signature detection','Exact matching against known malicious patterns.'],['Heuristics and behaviour','Judging by structure and by runtime actions.'],['Detection tradeoff','More coverage always means more false positives.']],sum:'Known threats are matched exactly, unknown ones are judged by behaviour, and the tradeoff is always false positives.'},

{t:'How facial filters work',c:'AI',m:11,hook:'The filter is not drawn on your face. {t:'How augmented reality works',c:'Technology',m:12,hook:'The hard part is not drawing the object. {t:'How virtual reality works',c:'Technology',m:12,hook:'VR is a race against your inner ear. It tracks head position and orientation with cameras and inertial sensors, and re-renders the scene from the new viewpoint. The critical number is motion-to-photon latency: the time from moving your head to the display showing the corresponding image. Above roughly 20 milliseconds, the mismatch between what your vestibular system reports and what your eyes see produces discomfort and nausea.','That deadline drives everything. Frame rates of 90 Hz or more, aggressive prediction of where your head will be when the frame is displayed, and timewarp techniques that re-project an already-rendered frame at the last moment to match the newest head pose. Lenses distort the image, so the renderer pre-distorts it inversely, and does so separately per colour channel to cancel chromatic aberration. Foveated rendering saves work by rendering at full detail only where the eye is actually looking, tracked in real time.'],ex:['Artificial locomotion causes far more sickness than real walking, because your body reports no motion while your eyes report plenty.','Teleport-style movement is common precisely because it avoids sustained visual motion without physical motion.'],facts:['Vergence-accommodation conflict — eyes converging at one distance while focusing at another — causes eye strain no current headset fully solves.','Rendering two eyes at high resolution and 90 Hz is several times the workload of an ordinary game at the same fidelity.'],keys:[['Motion-to-photon latency','The delay budget that governs comfort.'],['Timewarp','Re-projecting a rendered frame to the newest head pose.'],['Foveated rendering','Full detail only where the eye is looking.']],sum:'Track the head, render two eyes fast enough to beat the inner ear, and correct for the lenses on the way out.'},
{t:'How motion tracking works',c:'Technology',m:11,hook:'Every tracking system is a trade between something that drifts and something that is slow, fused into one estimate.',why:'The same principles run film effects, headsets, surgical robots and your phone\'s step counter.',body:['Optical tracking uses cameras to find known markers or natural features and triangulates position from multiple views. It is accurate and does not drift, but it fails on occlusion, needs line of sight, and updates only as fast as the cameras. Inertial tracking uses accelerometers and gyroscopes, which report at very high rates and never need to see anything — but they measure change, so errors accumulate and position drifts badly within seconds. Neither is sufficient alone.','So systems fuse them, typically with a Kalman filter that maintains an estimate plus its uncertainty and weights each new measurement by how reliable it is. Inertial data carries the estimate between camera frames and through brief occlusions; optical data corrects the accumulated drift whenever it is available. Outside-in systems place cameras in the room looking at the tracked object; inside-out systems put cameras on the object looking at the room, which is what modern standalone headsets use because it needs no external setup.'],ex:['Film motion capture uses reflective markers and many synchronised cameras, giving sub-millimetre accuracy in a prepared space.','A phone counting steps uses accelerometer patterns alone, which is why it can be fooled by shaking it.'],facts:['Gyroscope drift is why VR controllers left still for a long time slowly rotate away and need recentring.','Marker-free tracking now estimates full human pose from ordinary video using neural networks, at some cost in precision.'],keys:[['Optical tracking','Accurate and drift-free but needs line of sight.'],['Inertial tracking','Fast and self-contained but accumulates error.'],['Sensor fusion','Weighting each source by its reliability to get one estimate.']],sum:'Fast sensors that drift are corrected by slow sensors that do not, and fusion produces something better than either.'},
{t:'How game physics engines work',c:'Programming',m:12,hook:'A physics engine does not simulate reality. It simulates something that looks right and never explodes.',why:'The compromises explain every physics glitch you have ever laughed at.',body:['Each frame, the engine integrates forces to update velocities and positions, detects collisions, and resolves them. Collision detection is split for speed: a broad phase quickly rejects pairs that cannot possibly touch using bounding volumes, then a narrow phase computes exact contact points for the few surviving pairs. Resolution then applies impulses to separate overlapping bodies and produce plausible bounces, and a constraint solver enforces joints, limits and stacking.','The compromises are everywhere. Simulation runs at fixed timesteps for stability, decoupled from the render rate. Objects that come to rest are put to sleep so they stop consuming computation. Solvers are iterative and stop after a fixed number of passes, so a tall stack of boxes is never quite solved and jitters. Fast-moving objects can pass entirely through thin walls between timesteps unless continuous collision detection is enabled, which costs more. Every classic glitch — vibrating ragdolls, objects launched into orbit, a character stuck in geometry — comes from one of these approximations.'],ex:['Ragdolls flail when a constraint solver cannot satisfy conflicting joint limits and the error feeds back each frame.','Bullets are usually raycasts rather than physical objects, precisely because a fast small object would tunnel through walls.'],facts:['Physics is typically run at a fixed 50 or 60 Hz regardless of frame rate, with rendering interpolating between states.','Deterministic physics is surprisingly hard across machines, because floating-point results differ, which complicates multiplayer.'],keys:[['Broad and narrow phase','Cheap rejection before expensive exact collision tests.'],['Iterative solver','Approximate constraint resolution, stopped after fixed passes.'],['Tunnelling','Fast objects skipping through thin geometry between steps.']],sum:'Integrate, detect, resolve — all approximately and on a fixed clock, because stability matters more than accuracy.'},
{t:'How ray tracing works',c:'Programming',m:12,hook:'Instead of guessing what light would do, you follow individual rays backwards from the camera into the scene.',why:'It is how films have looked real for decades and why games only recently caught up.',body:['Traditional rasterisation projects triangles onto the screen and shades them with clever approximations — reflections faked with pre-rendered maps, shadows from depth buffers, indirect light baked in advance. Ray tracing inverts the problem. For each pixel, cast a ray from the camera into the scene, find what it hits, and then cast further rays from that point: toward light sources to test for shadow, in the mirror direction for reflections, and scattered in many directions to gather indirect light. Reflections, refraction, soft shadows and colour bleeding all emerge naturally rather than being special-cased.','The cost is enormous, because realistic indirect lighting needs many rays per pixel and the result is noisy with few. Two things made it viable in real time: dedicated hardware for testing ray intersections against a spatial acceleration structure, and machine-learning denoisers that reconstruct a clean image from a very sparse, noisy sample. Games therefore trace far fewer rays than a film would and lean heavily on denoising to make the difference.'],ex:['A mirror showing objects that are off-screen is the immediate visual giveaway of ray tracing, since screen-space reflections cannot show what is not rendered.','Caustics — light focused through glass or water — are nearly impossible to fake convincingly and appear naturally when traced.'],facts:['Film renderers have used path tracing for decades, spending hours per frame where a game has 16 milliseconds.','Bounding volume hierarchies make ray-scene intersection logarithmic rather than linear, which is what makes any of it tractable.'],keys:[['Ray casting','Following rays from the camera into the scene.'],['Global illumination','Indirect light emerging naturally from multiple bounces.'],['Denoising','Reconstructing a clean image from very few samples.']],sum:'Follow rays backwards from the eye and light behaves correctly by construction — at a cost only new hardware makes bearable.'},
{t:'How computer graphics work',c:'Programming',m:12,hook:'A GPU is not a fast processor. It is thousands of slow ones doing the same thing to different pixels.',why:'The architecture explains why graphics cards also run machine learning, and why some effects are cheap and others ruinous.',body:['Rendering a 3D scene means transforming triangles through a series of coordinate spaces — model, world, view, then projection onto the screen — then determining which pixels each triangle covers and what colour each should be. The transformations are matrix multiplications, applied identically to millions of vertices, and the shading is applied identically to millions of pixels. That uniformity is the key architectural fact: the same operation on vast amounts of independent data.','So GPUs are built as thousands of simple cores executing the same instruction on different data simultaneously, with memory bandwidth prioritised over latency. Programmable shader stages let developers write the vertex and pixel programmes, which is where all visual style lives. The same architecture happens to suit neural networks perfectly, since training is dominated by large matrix multiplications — which is why graphics hardware became the substrate of modern AI more or less by accident.'],ex:['A depth buffer resolves what is in front of what by storing the nearest distance seen at each pixel, discarding anything further.','Effects that need each pixel to know about its neighbours, like blur, cost far more than per-pixel effects because of memory access.'],facts:['A modern GPU has tens of thousands of cores against a CPU\'s handful, but each is far simpler and slower individually.','Branching code hurts GPU performance badly, because cores in a group must execute both branches when they disagree.'],keys:[['Parallelism','Thousands of cores applying one operation to many data points.'],['Shader programmes','Programmable vertex and pixel stages where style is defined.'],['Memory bandwidth','The usual bottleneck, prioritised over per-core speed.']],sum:'Graphics is the same arithmetic repeated across millions of independent points, which is exactly what a GPU is built for.'},
{t:'How AI image generation works',c:'AI',m:13,hook:'The model learns to remove noise from an image. Run that backwards from pure noise and a picture appears.',why:'Diffusion is counterintuitive, genuinely elegant, and now behind most image generation you see.',body:['Training works by destruction. Take a real image, add a small amount of random noise, and train a network to predict the noise that was added. Repeat across many noise levels until the network can denoise an image at any level of corruption. That is the entire training objective — no generation involved, just noise prediction.','Generation reverses it. Start with pure random noise and ask the model what noise to remove. Subtract its prediction, and you have something very slightly more image-like. Repeat twenty to fifty times and a coherent image emerges from nothing. Text control comes from conditioning: a text encoder converts the prompt into a vector, and the denoising network is trained to denoise toward images matching that vector, so the prompt steers every step. Most systems also work in a compressed latent space rather than on pixels, which cuts the computation enormously.'],ex:['Watching the intermediate steps shows a vague coloured blur resolving progressively into structure and then detail.','Inpainting works by fixing the known pixels and running the denoising process only on the masked region.'],facts:['Classifier-free guidance amplifies the difference between the prompted and unprompted prediction, which is the knob that trades fidelity to the prompt against image diversity.','Models struggle with text and hands because both demand precise global structure that a locally-denoising process finds hard to keep consistent.'],keys:[['Diffusion','Learning to remove noise, then running it backwards.'],['Conditioning','Steering the denoising with an encoded text prompt.'],['Latent space','Working on a compressed representation rather than raw pixels.']],sum:'Train a model to undo noise, then start from pure noise and undo it repeatedly until an image is there.'},
{t:'How AI voice generation works',c:'AI',m:12,hook:'Modern speech synthesis does not assemble recorded fragments. It predicts the waveform itself.',why:'It has crossed from obviously robotic to often indistinguishable, with consequences worth understanding.',body:['Older systems worked by concatenation: record a voice actor reading thousands of phrases, chop the audio into units, and splice them together. It sounded stitched because it was, and it could only say things whose parts had been recorded. Modern neural synthesis works in two stages instead. A model converts text into an intermediate acoustic representation — usually a mel spectrogram, a picture of how energy is distributed across frequencies over time — predicting rhythm, pitch and emphasis. A vocoder then turns that spectrogram into an actual waveform.','Voice cloning works because the speaker\'s identity can be separated from the content. A speaker encoder compresses a sample of someone\'s voice into a vector capturing timbre and style, and the synthesiser is conditioned on that vector, so it can say arbitrary text in that voice. Early systems needed hours of clean recording; current ones manage a recognisable clone from seconds. That is why voice is no longer usable as proof of identity, and why banks that once used voiceprints have had to add other checks.'],ex:['Audiobook and dubbing production increasingly uses synthesis with a licensed voice, with human direction for emphasis.','Fraud using cloned voices of relatives or executives is now common enough that agreed code words are standard advice.'],facts:['Prosody — rhythm and intonation — is what most reveals synthetic speech, since it depends on meaning rather than sound.','Detection of synthetic audio is unreliable and getting harder, which is why provenance and watermarking are being pursued instead.'],keys:[['Spectrogram prediction','Text converted to a time-frequency representation.'],['Vocoder','Turning that representation into an audible waveform.'],['Speaker embedding','A vector capturing a voice, separable from what is said.']],sum:'Predict the sound picture from the text, convert it to a waveform, and condition it on a vector that captures whose voice it is.'},
{t:'How credit cards make money',c:'Finance',m:11,hook:'The interest is not the main product. The main product is a fee the shop pays, taken out of what you spend.',why:'It explains rewards, why merchants hate certain cards, and who actually pays for your air miles.',body:['Every card transaction moves money through several parties. The merchant receives the sale minus a discount of roughly 1.5 to 3.5%, most of which goes to the bank that issued your card as an interchange fee. The card network takes a smaller cut, and the merchant\'s payment processor takes another. This happens on every purchase whether or not you ever pay a penny of interest, and it is a large, extremely reliable revenue stream.','Interest is the second stream, and it is enormous from the minority who revolve a balance. Rates are high because the debt is unsecured and can be run up instantly. Then come fees: annual fees, late fees, foreign transaction fees, cash advance fees which typically start accruing interest immediately with no grace period. Rewards are funded mainly out of interchange, which is why premium cards carry higher merchant fees — and why the cost is embedded in prices everyone pays, including those who pay in cash.'],ex:['Some merchants refuse premium rewards cards or add surcharges, because those cards carry the highest interchange rates.','Minimum payments are set low deliberately, since a balance carried for years is the most profitable customer outcome.'],facts:['Customers who pay in full every month are known in the industry as deadbeats, because they generate only interchange.','Interchange caps in the EU cut rates sharply, and rewards programmes there became noticeably less generous as a result.'],keys:[['Interchange fee','A percentage of every sale, paid by the merchant to your bank.'],['Revolving balance','Carried debt, where the high interest revenue comes from.'],['Cross-subsidy','Rewards funded by merchant fees embedded in all prices.']],sum:'Shops pay a cut of every sale, interest comes from those who carry a balance, and rewards are funded from the first.'},
{t:'How banks make money',c:'Finance',m:11,hook:'A bank does not lend out the deposits it holds. It creates new money when it makes a loan.',why:'The money-creation point is genuinely counterintuitive and is confirmed by central banks themselves.',body:['The core business is the net interest margin: pay depositors little, charge borrowers more, keep the difference. But the mechanism is not what most people picture. A bank does not take existing deposits and hand them to a borrower. When it approves a loan it credits the borrower\'s account with a new deposit, creating both an asset (the loan) and a liability (the deposit) simultaneously. The money did not exist before. Bank lending is where most of the money supply comes from, a point the Bank of England has published explicitly.','Constraints come from capital requirements, profitability and regulation rather than from a stock of deposits. Banks must hold capital against their assets so that losses fall on shareholders before depositors. Beyond lending, revenue comes from fees — accounts, cards, overdrafts, foreign exchange — and from investment banking, trading and wealth management at larger institutions. The structural fragility is that deposits are repayable on demand while loans are long-term, so a bank that is entirely solvent can still fail if enough depositors ask at once.'],ex:['Deposit insurance exists to stop rational individual withdrawals from becoming a collectively destructive run.','Silicon Valley Bank failed in 2023 because rising rates devalued its long-dated bonds and depositors withdrew faster than it could sell them.'],facts:['Physical cash is a small fraction of the money supply; most money is bank deposits that exist only as records.','Central banks influence lending mainly by setting the rate at which banks borrow, not by rationing deposits.'],keys:[['Net interest margin','The spread between what banks pay and charge.'],['Money creation','New deposits created by the act of lending.'],['Maturity transformation','Borrowing short and lending long, the source of run risk.']],sum:'Banks earn the spread between deposit and loan rates, and the loans themselves are how most money comes into existence.'},
{t:'How loans work',c:'Finance',m:11,hook:'The advertised interest rate is not what you pay. The schedule of when you repay decides that.',why:'Understanding amortisation and true cost is the difference between a good and a ruinous borrowing decision.',body:['A loan is money now in exchange for more money later. The lender prices it on three things: the risk you will not repay, the time the money is tied up, and expected inflation. Your credit history, income and any collateral all feed the first. Secured loans, backed by an asset the lender can seize, carry lower rates than unsecured ones for exactly that reason.','What matters practically is the repayment structure. In an amortising loan, each fixed payment covers the interest accrued since the last one, and whatever is left reduces the principal. Early on the balance is large, so most of your payment is interest and very little touches the debt. That flips gradually. This is why overpaying early has a disproportionate effect, and why extending the term reduces the monthly payment while substantially increasing the total paid. Always compare the APR, which includes fees, rather than the headline rate.'],ex:['On a 25-year mortgage, the first year\'s payments are overwhelmingly interest, and a single early overpayment removes decades of compounding.','Buy-now-pay-later products advertise zero interest but generate revenue from merchant fees and late charges instead.'],facts:['The rule of 72 gives a quick estimate: divide 72 by the interest rate to get the years for a debt to double if unpaid.','Payday loans quoted as a small percentage per fortnight translate to annual rates in the hundreds or thousands of percent.'],keys:[['Principal and interest','The amount borrowed versus the cost of borrowing it.'],['Amortisation','A schedule where early payments are mostly interest.'],['APR','The comparable rate including fees, not just the headline number.']],sum:'You pay for risk, time and inflation, and the repayment schedule decides how much of that you actually pay.'},
{t:'How mortgages work',c:'Finance',m:12,hook:'The bank owns your house until you finish paying, and that security is why the rate is so low.',why:'It is the largest financial commitment most people make, and small differences compound over decades.',body:['A mortgage is a secured loan: the property is collateral, so if you stop paying the lender can repossess and sell it. That security is why mortgage rates are far below credit card rates for the same borrower. The deposit matters because it determines the loan-to-value ratio — borrow 90% of the value and the lender has little cushion if prices fall, so the rate is higher; borrow 60% and the risk is small and the rate drops accordingly.','The main structural choice is fixed versus variable. Fixed rates lock the payment for a period, buying certainty at a small premium. Variable rates move with the central bank, which is cheaper when rates fall and painful when they rise. Because the term is so long, small rate differences dominate: on a typical 25-year loan, a single percentage point can change the total repaid by a substantial fraction of the original purchase price. Overpayments early in the term have the largest effect, since they remove interest that would have compounded for decades.'],ex:['Negative equity occurs when prices fall below the outstanding balance, which traps owners because selling does not clear the debt.','Remortgaging at the end of a fixed period is routine, since the lender\'s standard variable rate is usually far worse.'],facts:['Interest-only mortgages keep payments low but leave the entire principal outstanding at the end, requiring a separate repayment plan.','Lenders stress-test affordability against rates well above the current one, precisely because long loans outlive rate cycles.'],keys:[['Collateral','The property securing the loan, which is why rates are low.'],['Loan-to-value','How much you borrow against the value, which sets your rate.'],['Fixed vs variable','Paying a premium for payment certainty, or not.']],sum:'The house secures the loan, the deposit sets the rate, and over 25 years small rate differences become enormous ones.'},
{t:'How interest rates affect the economy',c:'Economics',m:12,hook:'Central banks have essentially one lever, and pulling it takes a year or more to be felt.',why:'It is the main tool of economic management, and the lag is why it so often looks mistimed.',body:['A central bank sets the rate at which it lends to commercial banks, and everything else follows. Raise it and borrowing becomes more expensive, so businesses postpone investment and households postpone large purchases. Saving becomes more attractive. Existing borrowers on variable rates have less to spend. Demand falls across the economy, and with it inflation. Cut the rate and every one of those runs in reverse.','Two complications dominate. First, the effect arrives with a long and variable lag — often twelve to eighteen months — so policymakers must act on forecasts, and the consequences of today\'s decision land after conditions have changed. Second, rates are a blunt instrument. They cannot distinguish inflation caused by excess demand from inflation caused by an oil shock or a broken supply chain, and raising rates does nothing about the latter except suppress the whole economy. Rates also move asset prices and exchange rates, which is why a change reverberates far beyond borrowing costs.'],ex:['Higher rates strengthen a currency, because foreign capital chases the better return, which cheapens imports and hurts exporters.','Bond prices fall when rates rise, because existing bonds paying less become worth less — the mechanism behind several 2023 bank failures.'],facts:['Central bank independence exists because governments face constant political pressure to keep rates low.','Near zero, the tool loses power, which is why quantitative easing was invented as a substitute.'],keys:[['Policy rate','The base rate from which other borrowing costs derive.'],['Transmission lag','Twelve to eighteen months before full effects appear.'],['Blunt instrument','One lever that cannot target the source of inflation.']],sum:'One rate changes the cost of borrowing everywhere, cooling or heating demand — with the effect arriving a year late.'},
{t:'How exchange rates work',c:'Economics',m:11,hook:'A currency is a product with a price, and that price is set by everyone who wants to buy or sell it.',why:'Exchange rates decide the cost of everything imported, and the mechanisms are widely misunderstood.',body:['Under a floating system the rate is simply where supply meets demand. Demand for a currency comes from anyone needing it: foreigners buying exports, tourists, and above all investors moving capital to buy assets denominated in it. Capital flows dwarf trade flows in volume, which is why interest rate expectations move currencies far more than trade balances do. Higher expected rates attract capital, raising the currency.','Some countries do not float. A peg fixes the rate to another currency, which imports that currency\'s stability but requires the central bank to defend the peg by buying or selling reserves, and surrenders independent monetary policy. Managed floats sit in between. The long-run anchor is purchasing power parity — the idea that identical goods should cost the same everywhere once converted — which holds poorly over years but roughly over decades, and explains why persistently high-inflation currencies depreciate.'],ex:['The Big Mac index compares the cost of one product worldwide as a rough purchasing power check.','Pegs break when defending them exhausts reserves, as in the 1992 collapse of the pound\'s position in the ERM.'],facts:['Foreign exchange turnover exceeds several trillion dollars a day, far beyond what trade requires.','A weaker currency helps exporters and tourism while making imports, including energy, more expensive.'],keys:[['Floating rate','Price set by supply and demand in the market.'],['Capital flows','Investment movements that dominate over trade flows.'],['Purchasing power parity','A long-run anchor based on comparable prices.']],sum:'Currencies are priced by everyone wanting to hold them, and investment flows move that price far more than trade does.'},
{t:'Why currencies rise and fall',c:'Economics',m:11,hook:'Currencies move on expectations, not on facts — by the time news is confirmed, the price already reflects it.',why:'It explains why good news sometimes weakens a currency and why predictions fail so consistently.',body:['Four forces dominate. Two problems govern the industry: adverse selection, where the people most likely to claim are keenest to buy, and moral hazard, where being insured changes behaviour. {t:'How supply chains work',c:'Business',m:12,hook:'A single product may cross a dozen borders before you see it, and the whole system runs on almost no inventory.',why:'Supply chains are invisible until they break, and 2020 showed how thin the margins for error are.',body:['A supply chain is the sequence turning raw materials into a delivered product: extraction, components, assembly, distribution, retail. Each stage is usually a different company in a different country, chosen for cost or capability. The dominant philosophy for decades has been just-in-time — hold minimal inventory and have components arrive exactly when needed. It frees enormous capital that would otherwise sit in warehouses, and it is efficient precisely because it removes slack.','Removing slack removes resilience, and small disturbances amplify. The bullwhip effect describes how a modest change in consumer demand becomes progressively larger swings upstream, as each tier orders extra to protect itself and the signal distorts. A single missing component halts an entire assembly line regardless of how available everything else is, which is what a shortage of cheap semiconductors did to car manufacturing. The response has been a shift toward holding buffer stock of critical items, dual sourcing, and moving some production closer to the customer.'],ex:['Car makers idled plants for months over chips worth a few dollars each, because no substitute could be qualified quickly.','One container ship blocking the Suez Canal for six days disrupted schedules globally for months afterwards.'],facts:['Global shipping moves the overwhelming majority of goods by volume, and container standardisation is what made it economic.','Many companies do not know who their second- and third-tier suppliers are, which is why disruptions surprise them.'],keys:[['Just-in-time','Minimal inventory, arriving exactly when needed.'],['Bullwhip effect','Demand changes amplifying at each step upstream.'],['Single point of failure','One missing component halting everything downstream.']],sum:'Efficiency came from removing slack, and every disruption since has been a bill for the slack that was removed.'},
{t:'How warehouses work',c:'Business',m:11,hook:'In a modern warehouse, similar items are deliberately not stored together.',why:'Chaotic storage is counterintuitive, provably better, and only possible because software tracks everything.',body:['Traditional warehouses group by category, which is intuitive and slow — a picker collecting an order walks between distant zones. Modern operations use chaotic or random storage instead: an item goes wherever there is space, its location is recorded, and the system directs pickers along an optimised route. It also spreads identical items across many locations, so several pickers can fetch the same product simultaneously without colliding, and demand spikes do not create a queue at one shelf.','Layout follows movement rate rather than category. Fast-moving goods sit near packing, slow movers further out, and the ranking is recalculated as demand shifts. Goods-to-person systems invert the problem entirely: robots carry mobile shelving units to a stationary picker, eliminating walking, which is the single largest cost in manual picking. Cross-docking skips storage altogether, moving inbound goods straight to outbound vehicles for products that turn over fast enough not to need holding at all.'],ex:['Amazon\'s robotic floors move whole shelf pods to workers, and human pickers never walk the aisles at all.','Supermarket distribution centres cross-dock fresh produce, which arrives and departs within hours and is never shelved.'],facts:['Walking is typically over half the time in manual order picking, which is why eliminating it is the main automation target.','Slotting — deciding where each item lives — is re-optimised continuously, since a good layout for last month is wrong this month.'],keys:[['Chaotic storage','Placing items anywhere and tracking locations in software.'],['Velocity slotting','Positioning by how fast items move, not by category.'],['Goods-to-person','Bringing shelves to the picker instead of the reverse.']],sum:'Store items anywhere and let software route the picker — or move the shelves and let nobody walk at all.'},
{t:'How logistics companies operate',c:'Business',m:11,hook:'The last mile of a delivery costs more than the ten thousand before it.',why:'It explains delivery fees, why free returns are unsustainable, and why route software matters so much.',body:['Moving goods long distances is cheap per unit because containers and freight aircraft carry enormous volume. {t:'How intellectual property works',c:'Business',m:12,hook:'Four different rights protect four different things, and confusing them is the most common and costly mistake.',why:'Nearly every modern business depends on intangible assets, and the rules differ sharply between types.',body:['Copyright protects the expression of an idea — text, code, music, images — automatically on creation, for the life of the author plus decades. Patents protect inventions: a new, useful and non-obvious technical solution, for around twenty years, in exchange for publishing exactly how it works.
